-th empirical quantile of the training residuals
\{|Y_{i}-\widehat{\mu}(X_{i})|\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

The test horizon is
T=10{,}000
time steps. We set the target miscoverage level to be
\alpha=0.1
. All experiments are repeated 40 times with different random seeds, and we report the mean and standard deviation of cumulative regret.

Numerical evaluation of cumulative regret.

We measure performance using the cumulative regret defined in Eqn. (
13
). However, since the true miscoverage probability
\mathbb{P}(s_{t}>{q}_{t}\mid{q}_{t})
is intractable, we estimate it via Monte Carlo simulation. Specifically, for each time step
t
, we pre-generate a fixed evaluation set of
M=500
independent samples
\{(X_{t}^{(m)},Y_{t}^{(m)})\}_{m=1}^{M}
from the true distribution
{\mathcal{D}}_{t}
at that time step. The instantaneous coverage rate (defined in Eqn. (
11
)) is then estimated as

\widehat{\mathsf{cvg}}_{t}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbbm{1}\left\{|Y_{t}^{(m)}-\widehat{\mu}(X_{t}^{(m)})|\leq q_{t}\right\}.

The cumulative regret up to time
T
is then calculated as

\widehat{\mathsf{Regret}}_{T}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\big|\widehat{\mathsf{cvg}}_{t}-(1-\alpha)\big|.

Methods for comparison.

We compare the following algorithms numerically.

•

DriftOCP
(
Algorithm

2
):
We use a drift detection threshold in
Algorithm

2
of
\sigma_{n,r}=4
. To avoid false positives from high-variance estimates, we require a minimum window size of
t\geq 10
before any drift detection can be declared.

•

ACI with decaying stepsizes
(
4
)
:

\eta_{t}=(t+1)^{-\gamma}
for
\gamma\in\{0.5,0.6\}
.

•

ACI with fixed stepsizes
(
39
)
:

\eta_{t}\equiv\eta\in\{0.01,0.1,0.5\}
.

Results.

Figure
3
summarizes both the regret and calibration dynamics across the four data-generating settings. The top row plots the cumulative regret over time, while the bottom row tracks the corresponding evolution of the calibration quantiles; the black dashed curve in the bottom row is an approximation of the ground-truth quantile, obtained via repeated simulations at each time point. Taken together, the two rows highlight the tuning trade-off of ACI: a large constant stepsize reacts quickly to distributional changes, but yields highly variable quantile trajectories even under stationarity, leading to substantial cumulative regret; conversely, smaller or decaying stepsizes stabilize the quantile updates in stationary periods, yet may adapt too slowly after distribution shifts and consequently lag behind the moving target (most notably in Setting 1).
As a result, the optimal stepsize for ACI differs across various settings,
making it difficult to select a priori in practice.
In contrast,
DriftOCP
adapts to different regimes in a data-driven manner,
achieving stable tracking during stationary segments and rapid re-alignment following change points.
This behavior leads to uniformly controlled regret across regimes, comparable to that attained by hindsight-optimal tuning of ACI.

Figure 3
:
Cumulative regret and calibration quantiles under four data-generating settings.

Top row: cumulative regret trajectories. Bottom row: calibration-quantile evolution; the black dashed curve indicates an approximation to the ground-truth quantile obtained via repeated simulations at each time point. Across settings, ACI exhibits a clear stepsize trade-off: ACI with large constant stepsizes adapts quickly but produces volatile quantile updates and suboptimal performance under stationarity, whereas ACI with smaller or decaying stepsizes yields more stable updates at the cost of slower adaptation to distributional changes. In comparison,
DriftOCP
is stable within stationary time segments and adapts rapidly to distribution shifts, yielding consistently controlled regret. Curves are averaged over 20 runs; shaded bands indicate
\pm 1
standard deviation.

5.2
Experiments: online conformal prediction with adaptively trained scores

This section examines how our methods interact with different ways of generating non-conformity scores in the presence of distribution drift.
In particular, we pair our drift-aware recalibration mechanism with (i) a
covariate-agnostic score, (ii) a fixed pretrained model, and (iii) an adaptively updated fitted model.
The numerical comparisons illustrate tangible gains from adaptively updated models
in terms of predictive efficiency and validity.

Data generation.

We consider an online regression stream
\{(X_{t},Y_{t})\}_{t\geq 1}

with feature dimension
d=10
.
Throughout these experiments, we construct prediction sets using a
linear
predictor, and the non-conformity score function is computed from linear regression residuals. We then consider two data-generating models for
(X_{t},Y_{t})
to distinguish well-specified learning from misspecification:

•

Well-specified case:

Y_{t}=X_{t}^{\top}\beta^{*}+\varepsilon_{t}
, with
\varepsilon_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, so the linear predictor can be correctly specified;

•

Misspecified case:

Y_{t}=X_{t}^{\top}\beta^{*}+\frac{1}{100}\|X_{t}\|_{2}^{2}+\varepsilon_{t}
, with

\varepsilon_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, where the additional quadratic term introduces a mild deviation from linearity.

In both cases, the true coefficient
\beta^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is sampled
from
\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and is subsequently held fixed across simulation repetitions.
For each model specification, we introduce piecewise-stationary covariate shifts with change points at

t\in\{3333,6667\}
. Specifically, we consider:

•

Mean shifts:

X_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{t}\mathbf{1}_{d},I_{d})
, where

\mu_{t}=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }t\leq 3333;\\
3,&\text{if }3333<t\leq 6667;\\
-2,&\text{if }t>6667;\end{cases}

•

Variance shifts:

X_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{t}^{2}I_{d})
, where

\sigma_{t}=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }t\leq 3333;\\
5,&\text{if }3333<t\leq 6667;\\
10,&\text{if }t>6667.\end{cases}

In each run of the experiments, we first draw
n_{\text{pretrain}}=100
independent observations to fit an initial
ridge regression model, and then draw an additional
n_{\text{train}}=500
observations
to initialize the calibration quantile for
the non-conformity scores. We subsequently generate an online data stream of length
T=10{,}000
.

Score construction strategies.

We compare three score construction strategies that are paired with
drift-aware recalibration. The first uses our full-conformal
variant tailored to online optimization, while the latter two
use
DriftOCP
with pretrained score functions:

•

DriftOCP
-
full
+ online SGD:
the score is formed using a sequentially updated fitted model,

s_{t}=|Y_{t}-X_{t}^{\top}\widehat{\beta}_{t}|
, where
\widehat{\beta}_{t}
is updated by online SGD with stepsize

\eta_{t}=0.01/\sqrt{t}
;

•

DriftOCP
+ pretrained ridge:
the score uses a fixed ridge-regression predictor
(
45
)
with regularization parameter
\lambda=1.0
, implemented in
scikit-learn

(
59
)
, as the pretrained model;

•

DriftOCP
+ absolute response:
a covariate-agnostic baseline
s_{t}=\left|{Y_{t}}\right|
.

All methods employ the same drift-detection threshold
\sigma=4
and the same doubling-round structure.
The target miscoverage level is
\alpha=0.1
for all settings.

Results.

We report the prediction interval width and the local coverage rate computed over a sliding window of 100 time
steps. As shown in
Figure

4
,
the results are averaged over 20 independent runs,
with shaded regions indicating
\pm 1
standard deviation.
The top row plots the prediction interval width over time,
while the bottom row shows the local coverage rate computed using a rolling window of 100 steps. The horizontal dashed line marks the target level

1-\alpha=0.9
and the vertical dashed lines correspond to
the change points.

Across all four settings—well-specified or misspecified models, and under either mean or variance drift—the adaptive-score method with online SGD consistently achieves the most favorable tradeoff, producing the narrowest intervals while maintaining stable coverage around the target level.
In contrast, the pretrained-score baseline tends to be sensitive to mismatches between the pretraining and test covariate distributions,
resulting in wider intervals and a higher degree of coverage fluctuations after the change points. The model-free baseline (
s_{t}=\left|{Y_{t}}\right|
) is in general conservative and
produces substantially wider intervals than the other two methods throughout.
It is also sensitive to distribution shift, exhibiting undercoverage at change points.

We also observe a transient effect at the beginning of the data stream: the adaptive method exhibits slightly biased local
coverage and inflated widths early on, which is as expected since the fitted model is still in its initialization phase and the online updates are relatively volatile. As more data arrive, the adaptively fitted model stabilizes, and the
resulting score becomes better calibrated, after which the method tracks the target coverage tightly even after
distributional shifts.

Finally, note that the adaptive method relies on an online SGD-trained predictor, whose trajectory depends on the data
order and thus does not satisfy permutation symmetry. The strong empirical performance of this non-symmetric learning
pipeline provides additional evidence supporting our training-conditional guarantees, which do not require symmetry of
the underlying fitted model.

Figure 4
:
Online conformal prediction with different score constructions.

Top row: prediction-interval width over time. Bottom row: local coverage rate computed with a rolling window of 100
steps; the horizontal line marks the target level
1-\alpha=0.9
. Vertical dashed lines indicate change points at

t=3333
and
t=6667
. Columns correspond to the four settings (well-specified vs. misspecified model, each under
mean vs. variance drift). The adaptive-score method (online SGD) yields noticeably shorter intervals and more stable
coverage under variance drift, whereas the pretrained-score method is sensitive to a mismatch between the pretraining
and test covariate distributions. The model-free baseline (
s_{t}=\left|{Y_{t}}\right|
) is in general conservative and produces wide prediction intervals; it is also
sensitive to distribution shift, exhibiting undercoverage at change points.
Curves are averaged over 20 runs, with shaded bands indicating
\pm 1
standard deviation.

6
Additional related work

In this section, we briefly discuss a small sample of other related papers.
To start with, a substantial body of work has established theoretical coverage guarantees for
conformal prediction
(
6
;
5
)
. The majority of these results, however, rely on the
assumption that the data are exchangeable (most notably, i.i.d. observations)
(e.g.,
76
;
79
;
51
;
13
).
A growing literature has investigated how conformal prediction procedures can be
modified to retain validity when exchangeability is violated, aiming to preserve meaningful coverage guarantees under various forms of distributional shift. For instance,

72
;
14
developed weighted split conformal methods
that restore
marginal
validity under a weighted-exchangeability condition. Their methods rely on importance weights tied to the data distribution; in a distribution-free setting, one must either use a non-data-dependent weight (as in
14
) or impose stronger structural assumptions (e.g., invariance of
Y\mid X
as in
(
72
)
).
In parallel,
60
;
67
developed split conformal
methods under label shift, when the marginal distribution of
Y
differs across environments
while the conditional distribution
P(X\mid Y)
remains invariant.
In another line of work
(
28
;
25
;
1
;
40
;
8
)
, distribution shifts between training and test environments are tackled from a distributionally robust optimization perspective.
Meanwhile, a growing literature developed conformal prediction methods for time-series data
(
87
;
83
;
85
;
84
;
82
;
27
;
30
;
69
)
.

For temporally dependent, non-exchangeable sequences,

57
showed that split conformal retains
approximate marginal
validity up to an explicit penalty term controlled by decoupling/mixing
conditions (including
\beta
-mixing), with sharper results subsequently obtained in
15
.
These results, however, do not yield sharp training-(and-calibration)-conditional concentration
bounds and typically require stationarity-type dependence assumptions.
In contrast, training-(and-calibration)-conditional coverage guarantees beyond exchangeability are comparatively rarer.

In addition, a line of work connected ACI-style calibration with ideas from online learning and studied regret bounds
under various performance criteria
(
19
;
37
;
88
;
63
;
55
)
, which, however, fell short of ensuring training-conditional coverage.
Moreover, the idea of ACI has been extended for broader settings, such as risk control
(
36
;
35
)
, stochastic control for time series
(
86
)
, and parametric quantile calibration
(
10
)
, among other things.

7
Discussion

In this work, we have developed two online conformal prediction methods that adapt efficiently to temporal distribution drift,
producing prediction sets that are both valid and informative. For scenarios involving pretrained score functions, our
DriftOCP
algorithm leverages an efficient drift detection subroutine to update the calibration set sequentially, achieving regret bounds that are minimax optimal (up to logarithmic factors) across both change-point and smooth drift regimes. For scenarios where the predictive models (and hence the score functions) are trained adaptively from prior observations, we propose
DriftOCP
-
full
, a full-conformal-style online algorithm that enjoys strong regret guarantees under stability assumptions; for this setting, we further establish matching minimax lower bounds under suitable restrictions on the prediction sets. Unlike much of the prior work that focused on metrics like empirical long-term coverage frequency or adversarial regret, our analysis exploits additional independence assumption across data while remaining otherwise distribution-free, which has enabled training-conditional regret guarantees. The proposed algorithms are horizon-free, computationally efficient, and supported by fully non-asymptotic, minimax-optimal theoretical guarantees.

Our results naturally suggest several directions for future investigation. To begin with, while our current theoretical development
hinges upon independence across data samples, many online predictive inference problems involve temporally dependent observations, as commonly encountered in, say, time-series settings
(
83
;
87
;
57
)
. A natural and
challenging direction is therefore to extend our online conformal methods to tackle temporally dependent, non-stationary Markovian environments, while still delivering rigorous training-conditional coverage guarantees.
On another front,
the training-conditional guarantees in
Section

4

rely on stability
assumptions for the underlying model-fitting algorithms. When the desirable stability conditions fail to hold or are difficult to verify—e.g., in certain nonparametric, or deep learning models
(
38
;
52
;
64
)
—it is fundamentally important to develop new online full conformal methods that ensure both validity and efficiency without stability. Finally, our algorithmic and analysis frameworks might shed light on other online statistical problems, such as online multicalibration
(
31
)
.

Appendix A
Proof of Fact
2.1

Proof of (i).

By the triangle inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{t}\in\mathcal{C}_{t})-(1-\alpha)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T}\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{t}\in\mathcal{C}_{t})-(1-\alpha)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T}\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t}}\left[\left|{{\mathbb{P}}\big(Y_{t}\in\mathcal{C}_{t}\mid{\mathcal{C}}_{t}\big)-(1-\alpha)}\right|\right]\leq\frac{\mathsf{regret}_{T}}{T}.

Proof of (ii).

For any time point
t
, let us construct
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}
as follows:

{\mathcal{C}}_{t}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\varnothing,&\text{with probability }\alpha,\\
{\mathbb{R}},&\text{with probability }1-\alpha.\end{cases}

On the one hand, it can be easily derived that, for any
1\leq t\leq T
,

{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{t}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{t})=\alpha\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{t}\in\varnothing\}+(1-\alpha)\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}\}=1-\alpha,

thus implying that

\mathsf{lt\text{-}cvg}_{T}=\frac{1}{T}\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{t}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{t})=1-\alpha.

On the other hand, it is seen that

\displaystyle\left|{{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{t}\in\varnothing)-(1-\alpha)}\right|=1-\alpha;

\displaystyle\left|{{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}})-(1-\alpha)}\right|=\left|{1-(1-\alpha)}\right|=\alpha.

Therefore, the following holds naturally

\left|{{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{t}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{t}\mid{\mathcal{C}}_{t})-(1-\alpha)}\right|\geq\min\{1-\alpha,\alpha\}=\alpha.

Summing over all
t=1,\dots,T
and recalling the definition of
\mathsf{regret}_{T}
, we complete the proof.

Appendix B
Detailed proofs in
Section

3

Before proceeding, let us introduce some convenient notation.

B.1
Proof of Theorem
3.1

We now turn to the proof of
Theorem

3.1
. At a high level, we shall first establish a per-round regret bound, then aggregate these
bounds within each stage, and finally sum over all stages to obtain regret bounds over the entire time horizon
[T]
.

B.1.1
Additional notation

To facilitate presentation for the analysis of Algorithm
2
, we introduce some additional notation. First, write

s_{n,r,l}=s_{n,r,l}(X_{n,r,l},Y_{n,r,l})\qquad\text{and}\qquad s_{t}=s_{t}(X_{t},Y_{t})

as long as it is clear from the context.
In addition, for any
1\leq i<j\leq T
, we define the “typical” event:

{\mathcal{A}}(i,j)\coloneqq\left\{\sup\limits_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}\left\{\left|{\sum\limits_{{t}={i}}^{j}\big(\mathbbm{1}\{s_{t}\leq x\}-{\mathbb{P}}(s_{t}\leq x)\big)}\right|\right\}\leq 6\sqrt{(j-i+1)\log j}\right\}.

(37a)

To see that this is a high-probability event, invoking
Lemma

E.4
with
\delta=j^{-6}
and using the fact

\frac{4}{\sqrt{j-i+1}}+\sqrt{\frac{6\log j}{2(j-i+1)}}\leq 4\sqrt{\frac{\log j}{j-i+1}}+\sqrt{\frac{3\log j}{j-i+1}}<6\sqrt{\frac{\log j}{j-i+1}},

we can establish that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\big({\mathcal{A}}(i,j)^{\mathrm{c}}\big)\leq j^{-6}.

(37b)

Moreover, for any stage-round pair
(n,r)
, we define

{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}\coloneqq\bigcap\limits_{i=\tau_{n,r}}^{\tau_{n,r+1}-1}\bigcap\limits_{j=i+1}^{\tau_{n,r+1}-1}{\mathcal{A}}(i,j),

(37c)

where we recall that
\tau_{n,r}
indicates the time at which round
r
of stage
n
begins and
\tau_{n,r_{n}+1}=\tau_{n+1,1}
. For convenience, we also write

\displaystyle\tau_{n}\coloneqq\tau_{n,1}.

(38)

Moreover, we introduce several notations for the change-point setting. Recall that under the change-point model,
the entire horizon
[T]
is partitioned into
N^{\mathsf{cp}}+1
time segments,
within each of which the score distributions remain fixed.

Definition B.1
.

We define the following notation:

•

\mathcal{I}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{I}_{N^{\mathsf{cp}}+1}
: the
N^{\mathsf{cp}}+1
time segments over the entire horizon;

•

K_{n,r}
: the total number of time segments in round
r
of stage
n
;

•

S_{n,r}
and
t_{n}
: the total number of iterations in round
r
of stage
n
;

in particular, let
t_{n}=S_{n,r_{n}}
, i.e., the number of iterations in the last round of this stage;

•

\mathcal{I}_{n,r,k}

(k=1,\cdots,K_{n,r})
: the
k
-th time segment in round
r
of stage
n
;

•

S_{n}
: the total number of iterations in stage
n
;

•

J_{n}
: the total number of time segments in stage
n
;

•

\mathcal{I}_{n,j}

(j=1,\cdots,J_{n})
: the
j
-th time segment in stage
n
.

Also, for any time segment
\mathcal{I}
, we let
|\mathcal{I}|
represent the length of this time segment. In addition, while the last round
r_{n}
of stage
n
contains
t_{n}\leq T_{r_{n}}
iterations, we generate—for convenience of presentation—a set of random variables
\{s_{n,r,l}\}
for
l>t_{n}
in a way that obeys

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})=\alpha\qquad\text{for all }l>t_{n}.

(39)

Moreover, for round
r
in stage
n
, we define the cumulative KS distance within this round as

\displaystyle\mathsf{KS}_{n,r}^{\mathsf{round}}\coloneqq\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{S_{n,r}-1}\mathsf{KS}(s_{n,r,l},s_{n,r,l+1}).

(40a)

We also define the cumulative KS distance within stage
n
(which contains
r_{n}
rounds) as

\displaystyle\mathsf{KS}_{n}^{\mathsf{stage}}\coloneqq\sum_{r=1}^{r_{n}}\mathsf{KS}_{n,r}^{\mathsf{round}}.

(40b)

B.1.2
Decomposing and bounding the cumulative regret

In order to bound the cumulative regret,
we first decompose it based on stages and rounds of
DriftOCP
as well as the typical events
\{\mathcal{A}_{n,r}\}
introduced in
Section

B.1.1
:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{t}>q_{t}\mid q_{t})-\alpha\big|

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N}\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}}\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}\}

(41)

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N}\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}}\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}^{\mathrm{c}}\}.

As it turns out, the first term on the right-hand side of (
41
) serves as the dominant term, as argued below.

Consider any time point
t
belonging to round
r
of stage
n
. According to the procedure of
DriftOCP
—particularly the fact that the rounds length grow geometrically with
T_{r}=3^{r}
—it follows that neither
t/4
nor
4t
lies within the same round
(n,r)
, and as a result,

{\mathcal{E}}_{t}\coloneqq\bigcap\limits_{j=t/4}^{4t}\bigcap\limits_{i=1}^{j}{\mathcal{A}}(i,j)\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}.

It can thus be seen from (
37b
) that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}}_{t}^{\mathrm{c}})

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{{j}={t/4}}^{4t}\sum\limits_{{i}={1}}^{j}{\mathbb{P}}\big({\mathcal{A}}(i,j)^{\mathrm{c}}\big)\leq\sum\limits_{{j}={t/4}}^{4t}\sum\limits_{{i}={1}}^{j}\frac{1}{j^{4}}\leq\sum\limits_{{j}={t/4}}^{4t}\frac{1}{j^{3}}\leq\frac{32}{t^{2}},

which helps us control the second term on the right-hand side of (
41
) as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N}\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}}\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big|\,\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}^{\mathrm{c}}\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{t}>q_{t}\mid q_{t})-\alpha\big|\,\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{E}}_{t}^{\mathrm{c}}\}\right]

(42)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}{\mathbb{P}}\big({\mathcal{E}}_{t}^{\mathrm{c}}\big)\leq\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}\frac{32}{t^{2}}=O(1).

As a consequence, the remainder of this proof is devoted primarily to bounding the first term of (
41
). Towards this end, we begin by looking at the cumulative coverage gaps in round
r
of stage
n
. Informally, in the change-point setting, the cumulative coverage gap over this round on the typical events defined in
Section

B.1.1
is upper bounded by a sum of square-root terms in the lengths of the time segments. In contrast, under smooth drift, the bound contains a term that scales with a suitable KS distance raised to the

1/3
power, in a manner that resembles the final regret bound in
Theorem

3.1
. This is stated in the following lemma, with the proof deferred to
Section

B.3.1
.

Lemma B.1
.

Consider any stage-round pair
(n,r)
in Algorithm
2
. If no distribution shift has been detected by the subroutine
DriftDetect
by the end of this round, then it holds that

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r}}\Big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\Big|\bigg)\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}\}=\begin{cases}\widetilde{O}\bigg(\sum\limits_{{k}={1}}^{K_{n,r}}\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r,k}}\right|}\bigg),&\text{for the change-point setting;}\\
\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{T_{r}}+(\mathsf{KS}_{n,r}^{\mathsf{round}})^{\frac{1}{3}}T_{r}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right),&\text{for the smooth drift setting.}\end{cases}

Owing to the doubling trick employed in
DriftOCP
(i.e., the round lengths grow geometrically), we can lift the per-round cumulative gap bound in
Lemma

B.1
to a per-stage cumulative gap bound, again on the typical events defined in
Section

B.1.1
. This result is formalized in the following lemma, whose proof is postponed to
Section

B.3.2
.

Lemma B.2
.

Consider any stage
n
in Algorithm
2
, which comprises
r_{n}
rounds.
Then we have

\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}}\bigg(\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r}}\Big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\Big|\bigg)\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}\}=\begin{cases}\widetilde{O}\bigg(\sum\limits_{{j}={1}}^{J_{n}}\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,j}}\right|}\bigg),~&\text{for the change-point setting};\\
\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{S_{n}}+(\mathsf{KS}_{n}^{\mathsf{stage}})^{\frac{1}{3}}S_{n}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right),~&\text{for the smooth drift setting}.\end{cases}

It remains to see how the per-stage cumulative regret bounds derived above can be leveraged to establish Theorem
3.1
. To this end, we cope with the change-point and smooth drift settings separately in what follows.

B.1.3
Controlling the dominant term in the change-point setting

Recall the definition of
\{\mathcal{I}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N^{\textsf{cp}}+1}
and
\{\mathcal{I}_{n,j}\}_{j=1}^{J_{n}}
in
Section

B.1.1
, and
denote

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{n}=\mathcal{A}_{n,r_{n}-1}\cap\mathcal{A}_{n,r_{n}}.

(43)

Suppose that
DriftOCP
contains
N
stages.
Applying
Lemma

B.2
tells us that

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{r_{n}}\sum_{l=1}^{T_{r}}

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{P}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big|\,\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{A}_{n,r}\}\leq\widetilde{O}\Bigg(\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{J_{n}}\sqrt{|\mathcal{I}_{n,j}|}\bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{O}\Bigg(\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{J_{n}}\sqrt{|\mathcal{I}_{n,j}|}\bigg)\,\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}+\sum_{n=1}^{N}(\tau_{n+1}-\tau_{n})\,\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\}\Bigg),

(44)

where the second relation follows since, for any
n\in[N]
,

\sum\limits_{{j}={1}}^{J_{n}}\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,j}}\right|}\leq\sqrt{J_{n}\bigg(\sum\limits_{{j}={1}}^{J_{n}}\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,j}}\right|\bigg)}=\sqrt{J_{n}(\tau_{n+1}-\tau_{n})}\leq\tau_{n+1}-\tau_{n}.

In the sequel, we bound the two terms on the right-hand side of (
44
) separately.

•

To bound the first term on the right-hand side of (
44
), we first show that on the typical events, the final two rounds of any stage do not share exactly the same score distributions. This is formally stated in the lemma below, whose proof is provided in
Section

B.3.3
.

Lemma B.3
.

Consider any stage
n
in the change-point setting.
On the event

{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r_{n}-1}\cap{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r_{n}}
, the rounds
r_{n}-1
and
r_{n}
cannot both be entirely contained within the same time segment from
\{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N^{\mathsf{cp}}+1}
.

In view of
Lemma

B.3
, each time segment
\mathcal{I}_{k}
cannot overlap with more than two consecutive stages; in other words, each
\mathcal{I}_{k}
can contain at most two time segments from

\{\mathcal{I}_{n,j}:\ n\in[N],\ j\in[J_{n}]\}
. As a consequence,

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{J_{n}}\sqrt{|\mathcal{I}_{n,j}|}\Big)\,\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}\leq 2\sum_{k=1}^{N^{\textsf{cp}}+1}\sqrt{|\mathcal{I}_{k}|}\leq 2\sqrt{(N^{\textsf{cp}}+1)T},

where the last step follows from Cauchy-Schwarz as well as the fact that
\sum_{k=1}^{N^{\mathsf{cp}}+1}|\mathcal{I}_{k}|=T
.

•

Turning to the second term on the right-hand side of (
44
), we make note of the decomposition:

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N}(\tau_{n+1}-\tau_{n})\mathbbm{1}\left\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\right\}\right]\leq\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\tau_{n+1}-\tau_{n})\mathbbm{1}\left\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\right\}\right]

(45)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{i<j}(j-i){\mathbb{P}}\left(\tau_{n}=i,\tau_{n+1}=j,{\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{i<j}(j-i){\mathbb{P}}(\tau_{n}=i){\mathbb{P}}\left(\bigcup\limits_{k=i\vee\frac{j}{16}}^{j}\bigcup\limits_{l=i\vee\frac{j}{16}}^{k-1}{\mathcal{A}}(l,k)^{\mathrm{c}},\tau_{n+1}=j~\Big|~\tau_{n}=i\right),

where the last inequality uses a simple property of
DriftOCP
, namely,
16\tau_{n,r_{n}-1}\geq\tau_{n+1}
due to the exponential growth of round lengths. Additionally, observe that under the independent data assumption, for any
l,k\in[i,j]
the event
{\mathcal{A}}(l,k)^{\mathrm{c}}
is independent of what has happened prior to time
i
, which taken together with (
37b
) and the union bound gives

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\Bigg(\bigcup\limits_{k=i\vee\frac{j}{16}}^{j}\bigcup\limits_{l=i\vee\frac{j}{16}}^{k-1}{\mathcal{A}}(l,k)^{\mathrm{c}},\tau_{n+1}=j~\Big|~\tau_{n}=i\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{{k}={i\vee\frac{j}{16}}}^{j}\sum\limits_{{l}={i\vee\frac{j}{16}}}^{k-1}{\mathbb{P}}\big({\mathcal{A}}(l,k)^{\mathrm{c}}\mid\tau_{n}=i\big)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{{k}={i\vee\frac{j}{16}}}^{j}\sum\limits_{{l}={i\vee\frac{j}{16}}}^{k-1}{\mathbb{P}}\big({\mathcal{A}}(l,k)^{\mathrm{c}}\big)\leq\sum\limits_{{k}={i\vee\frac{j}{16}}}^{j}\sum\limits_{{l}={i\vee\frac{j}{16}}}^{k-1}\frac{1}{k^{6}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{{k}={i\vee\frac{j}{16}}}^{j}\frac{1}{k^{5}}<\frac{1}{4\left(i\vee\frac{j}{16}\right)^{4}}.

Substituting this bound into (
45
) results in

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N}(\tau_{n+1}-\tau_{n})\mathbbm{1}\left\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{i<j}(j-i){\mathbb{P}}(\tau_{n}=i)\frac{1}{4\left(i\vee\frac{j}{16}\right)^{4}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{{i}={1}}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}(\tau_{n}=i)\left\{\sum\limits_{{j}={i+1}}^{16i}\frac{j-i}{i^{4}}+\sum\limits_{{j}={16i+1}}^{\infty}\frac{j-i}{j^{4}}\right\}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{{i}={1}}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}(\tau_{n}=i)\frac{1}{i^{2}}\overset{\mathrm{(a)}}{\leq}\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{{i}={1}}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}(\tau_{n}=i)\frac{1}{n^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{2}}=O(1),

where (a) relies on the elementary bound
\tau_{n}\geq n
.

Putting the preceding bounds together, we arrive at

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N}\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}}\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}\}\right]\leq 2\sqrt{(N^{\textsf{cp}}+1)T}+O(1).

The advertised bound in the change-point setting then follows by combining this with (
42
).

B.1.4
Controlling the dominant term in the smooth drift setting

From Lemma
B.2
, we have established how to bound the cumulative regret within a single stage. As before, suppose there are
N
stages in total. Summing over the stage index
n
then yields the following bound:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N}\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}}\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N}\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{S_{n}}+\big(\mathsf{KS}^{\textsf{stage}}_{n}\big)^{\frac{1}{3}}S_{n}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(a)}}{\leq}\widetilde{O}\left(\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\sqrt{S_{n}}+\sqrt{T}+\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N}\big(\mathsf{KS}^{\textsf{stage}}_{n}\big)^{\frac{1}{3}}S_{n}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(b)}}{\leq}\widetilde{O}\left(\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\sqrt{S_{n}}+\sqrt{T}+\bigg(\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N}\mathsf{KS}^{\textsf{stage}}_{n}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{3}}\bigg(\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N}S_{n}\bigg)^{\frac{2}{3}}\right)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{O}\left(\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\sqrt{S_{n}}+\sqrt{T}+(\mathsf{KS}_{T})^{\frac{1}{3}}T^{\frac{2}{3}}\right).

(46)

Here, (a) holds since
\sqrt{S_{N}}\leq\sqrt{T}
, whereas (b) results from Hölder’s inequality.

With the above inequality in mind, a crucial task is to bound
\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\sqrt{S_{n}}
, which can be decomposed into

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\sqrt{S_{n}}=\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\sqrt{S_{n}}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}+\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\sqrt{S_{n}}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\}

(47)

with
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
denoting the event
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}={\mathcal{A}}_{n,r_{n-1}}\cap{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r_{n}}
(see (
43
)).
By applying an argument analogous to the one used to control the second term in (
44
), we can readily obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\sqrt{S_{n}}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\}\right]=O(1),

(48)

where we omit the details for brevity. Therefore, everything comes down to controlling

\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\sqrt{S_{n}}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}
,
which forms the main content of the remainder of this subsection.

For
n\in[N-1]
, note that the last round
r_{n}
of each of these stages ends with a restart; that is, a drift detection is declared in round
r_{n}
. Following the proof of Lemma
B.2
, let
t_{n}
denote the number of iterations in round
r_{n}
, then according to the drift detection subroutine, there exists some
j_{n}\in[t_{n}]
such that

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum\limits_{{l}={j_{n}}}^{t_{n}}\left(\mathbbm{1}\{s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\}-\alpha\right)\bigg|>24\sqrt{(t_{n}-j_{n}+1)\log(4\tau_{n,r_{n}})}.

(49)

On the event

{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
,
it is observed that

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum\limits_{{l}={j_{n}}}^{t_{n}}\left({\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-\alpha\right)\bigg|

(50)

\displaystyle\geq\bigg|\sum\limits_{{l}={j_{n}}}^{t_{n}}\big(\mathbbm{1}\{s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\}-\alpha\big)\bigg|-\bigg|\sum\limits_{{l}={j_{n}}}^{t_{n}}\big(\mathbbm{1}\{s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\}-{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})\big)\bigg|

\displaystyle>24\sqrt{(t_{n}-j_{n}+1)\log(4\tau_{n,r_{n}})}-6\sqrt{(t_{n}-j_{n}+1)\log\tau_{n+1}}>18\sqrt{(t_{n}-j_{n}+1)\log\tau_{n+1}},

where the last line arises from (
49
) and the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{n,r_{n}}
.
Further, let us introduce

B_{n}\coloneqq\frac{1}{t_{n}-j_{n}+1}\sum\limits_{{l}={j_{n}}}^{t_{n}}\big({\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-\alpha\big),

which, according to Eqn. (
50
), satisfies

\frac{\sqrt{t_{n}-j_{n}+1}}{18\sqrt{\log\tau_{n+1}}}\left|{B_{n}}\right|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}\geq\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}.

(51)

With this inequality in place, we can readily obtain

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\sqrt{S_{n}}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}\overset{\eqref{eq:split_regret_3}}{\leq}\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\sqrt{S_{n}}\left(\frac{\sqrt{t_{n}-j_{n}+1}}{18\sqrt{\log\tau_{n+1}}}\left|{B_{n}}\right|\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}S_{n}^{\frac{2}{3}}\left|{B_{n}}\right|^{\frac{1}{3}}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}\leq\bigg(\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}S_{n}\bigg)^{\frac{2}{3}}\bigg(\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\left|{B_{n}}\right|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{3}}\leq T^{\frac{2}{3}}\bigg(\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\left|{B_{n}}\right|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{3}},

(52)

where the last line follows from
Hölder’s inequality and Jensen’s inequality. Thus, it amounts to bounding
\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\left|{B_{n}}\right|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}
, which we accomplish next.

For every
B_{n}
, it follows from the triangle inequality that

\displaystyle\left|{B_{n}}\right|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\frac{1}{t_{n}-j_{n}+1}\sum\limits_{{l}={j_{n}}}^{t_{n}}\big({\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-\alpha\big)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\bigg|\frac{1}{t_{n}-j_{n}+1}\sum\limits_{{l}={j_{n}}}^{t_{n}}\bigg({\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-\frac{1}{T_{r_{n}-1}}\sum\limits_{{i}={1}}^{T_{r_{n}-1}}{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n}-1,i}^{\prime}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})\bigg)\bigg|}_{\eqqcolon{\mathcal{T}}_{n,1}}

\displaystyle~~~~~+\underbrace{\bigg|\frac{1}{T_{r_{n}-1}}\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r_{n}-1}}\big({\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n}-1,l}^{\prime}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-\mathbbm{1}\{s_{n,r_{n}-1,l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\}\big)\bigg|}_{\eqqcolon{\mathcal{T}}_{n,2}}

\displaystyle~~~~~+\underbrace{\bigg|\frac{1}{T_{r_{n}-1}}\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r_{n}-1}}\big(\mathbbm{1}\{s_{n,r_{n}-1,l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\}-\alpha\big)\bigg|}_{\eqqcolon{\mathcal{T}}_{n,3}},

where, for each
i=1,\ldots,T_{r_{n}-1}
,
s^{\prime}_{n,r_{n}-1,i}
is an independent copy of
s_{n,r_{n}-1,i}
.
The above inequality reduces the problem to controlling three terms.

•

Regarding
{\mathcal{T}}_{n,1}
, one can insert
{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},1}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})
into each summand to derive

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{n,1}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg|\frac{1}{t_{n}-j_{n}+1}\sum\limits_{{l}={j_{n}}}^{t_{n}}\big({\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},1}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})\big)\bigg|

(53)

\displaystyle+\bigg|\frac{1}{T_{r_{n}-1}}\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r_{n}-1}}\big({\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},1}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n}-1,l}^{\prime}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})\big)\bigg|.

For any
l\in[j_{n},t_{n}]
, we can apply the telescoping technique and the triangle inequality to obtain

\displaystyle\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},1}>\,

\displaystyle q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})\big|\leq\sup\limits_{q\in{\mathbb{R}}}\left\{\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},l}>q)-{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},1}>q)\big|\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{{j}={1}}^{l-1}\sup\limits_{q\in{\mathbb{R}}}\left\{\Big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},j+1}>q)-{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},j}>q)\Big|\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{{j}={1}}^{l-1}\mathsf{KS}(s_{n,r_{n},j},s_{n,r_{n},j+1})\leq\sum\limits_{{j}={1}}^{t_{n}-1}\mathsf{KS}(s_{n,r_{n},j},s_{n,r_{n},j+1});

Repeating the same arguments and adopting the notation
s_{n,r_{n}-1,T_{r_{n}-1}+1}\coloneqq s_{n,r_{n},1}
also give

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},1}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n}-1,l}^{\prime}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})\leq\sum\limits_{{j}={1}}^{T_{r_{n}-1}}\mathsf{KS}(s_{n,r_{n}-1,j},s_{n,r_{n}-1,j+1}).

Substituting these two results into Eqn. (
53
) yields

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{n,1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{t_{n}-j_{n}+1}\sum\limits_{{l}={j_{n}}}^{t_{n}}\sum\limits_{{j}={1}}^{t_{n}-1}\mathsf{KS}(s_{n,r_{n},j},s_{n,r_{n},j+1})+\frac{1}{T_{r_{n}-1}}\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r_{n}-1}}\sum\limits_{{j}={1}}^{T_{r_{n}-1}}\mathsf{KS}(s_{n,r_{n}-1,j},s_{n,r_{n}-1,j+1})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{{j}={1}}^{T_{r_{n}-1}}\mathsf{KS}(s_{n,r_{n}-1,j},s_{n,r_{n}-1,j+1})+\sum\limits_{{j}={1}}^{t_{n}-1}\mathsf{KS}(s_{n,r_{n},j},s_{n,r_{n},j+1}),

where we let
r_{n}-1
represent round
r_{n-1}
of stage
n-1
if
r_{n}=1
. Therefore, when summing over
n
, each KS distance term is counted at most twice, and as a result,

\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{n,1}\leq 2\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T-1}\mathsf{KS}(s_{t},s_{t+1})=2\mathsf{KS}_{T}.

•

With regards to
{\mathcal{T}}_{n,2}
, on the event
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
(cf. (
43
)), we can from the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{n,r_{n}-1}
(cf. (
37
)) that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{n,2}=\bigg|\frac{1}{T_{r_{n}-1}}\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r_{n}-1}}\big({\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n}-1,l}^{\prime}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-\mathbbm{1}\{s_{n,r_{n}-1,l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\}\big)\bigg|\leq 6\sqrt{\frac{\log\tau_{n+1}}{T_{r_{n}-1}}},

which in turn implies that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{n,2}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}6\sqrt{\frac{\log\tau_{n+1}}{T_{r_{n}-1}}}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:split_regret_3}}{\leq}\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}6\sqrt{\frac{\log\tau_{n+1}}{T_{r_{n}-1}}}\left(\frac{\sqrt{t_{n}}}{18\sqrt{\log\tau_{n+1}}}\left|{B_{n}}\right|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}\right)\leq\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\frac{2}{3}\left|{B_{n}}\right|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}.

Here, the last inequality follows from the fact that
t_{n}\leq T_{r_{n}}\leq 4T_{r_{n}-1}
for
r_{n}>1
and
t_{n}\leq T_{r_{n}}=1\leq T_{r_{n}-1}
for
r_{n}=1
.

•

When it comes to
{\mathcal{T}}_{n,3}
, it is seen from the definition of
q_{n,r}
—which is chosen to be the
\alpha
-empirical-quantile of
\{s_{n,r_{n}-1,l}\}_{l=1}^{T_{r_{n}-1}}
—that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{n,3}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}

\displaystyle=\bigg|\frac{1}{T_{r_{n}-1}}\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r_{n}-1}}\big(\mathbbm{1}\{s_{n,r_{n}-1,l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\}-\alpha\big)\bigg|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T_{r_{n}-1}}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}\leq\frac{3\sqrt{\log\tau_{n+1}}}{2\sqrt{t_{n}-j_{n}+1}}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}\overset{\eqref{eq:split_regret_3}}{\leq}\frac{1}{12}|B_{n}|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}.

Taking together the preceding bounds on
{\mathcal{T}}_{n,1}
,
{\mathcal{T}}_{n,2}
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{n,3}
results in

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\left|{B_{n}}\right|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}({\mathcal{T}}_{n,1}+{\mathcal{T}}_{n,2}+{\mathcal{T}}_{n,3})\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathsf{KS}_{T}+\frac{2}{3}\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\left|{B_{n}}\right|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}+\frac{1}{12}\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\left|{B_{n}}\right|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}=2\mathsf{KS}_{T}+\frac{3}{4}\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}|B_{n}|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\},

from which it follows that

\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\left|{B_{n}}\right|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}\leq 8\mathsf{KS}_{T}.

Combine this bound with (
52
) to arrive at

\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\sqrt{S_{n}}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}\leq 2(\mathsf{KS}_{T})^{\frac{1}{3}}T^{\frac{2}{3}}.

(54)

To finish up, taking (
46
), (
48
) and (
54
) collectively yields

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N}\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}}\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r}}

\displaystyle\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}\}\bigg]\leq\widetilde{O}\Bigg({\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\sqrt{S_{n}}\bigg]+\sqrt{T}+\mathsf{KS}_{T}^{\frac{1}{3}}T^{\frac{2}{3}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{O}\bigg({\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\sqrt{S_{n}}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\}\bigg]+{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sum\limits_{{n}={1}}^{N-1}\sqrt{S_{n}}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\}\bigg]+\sqrt{T}+(\mathsf{KS}_{T})^{\frac{1}{3}}T^{\frac{2}{3}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:split_regret_5}}{\leq}\widetilde{O}\big(\sqrt{T}+(\mathsf{KS}_{T})^{\frac{1}{3}}T^{\frac{2}{3}}\big).

We can immediately finish the proof for the smooth drift setting by combining the above result with (
42
).

B.2
Proof of
Theorem

3.2

Consider a given
T
, along with a change-point budget
N^{\mathsf{cp}}
in the change-point setting and a cumulative variation budget
\mathsf{KS}_{T}
in the smooth drift setting. In what follows, we intend to construct a subclass of distributions
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
and use it to establish the claimed minimax lower bound.

Step 1: construction of a distribution subclass
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
.

Partition the horizon
[T]\coloneqq\{1,\dots,T\}
into
m
consecutive time segments

{\mathcal{I}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{I}}_{m}
of (nearly) equal size, where

{\mathcal{I}}_{j}\;\coloneqq\;\bigl\{(j-1)\lceil T/m\rceil+1,\ \dots,\ \min\{j\lceil T/m\rceil,\,T\}\bigr\},\qquad j\in[m].

Define
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
as the collection of distribution sequences whose corresponding score distributions
\{\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{score}}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
obey

1.

for each
t\in[T]
,
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{score}}_{t}\in\{\mathrm{Exp}(1),\mathrm{Exp}(1+\varepsilon)\}
, where
\mathrm{Exp}(\beta)
denotes the exponential distribution with the rate parameter
\beta
, and the parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
will be specified momentarily;

2.

{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{score}}_{t}
is blockwise constant, namely, for each
j\in[m]
, one has
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{score}}_{t}={\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{score}}_{t^{\prime}}
for all
t,t^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{j}
.

Clearly, if
m=N^{\mathsf{cp}}+1
, then
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{1}(N^{\mathsf{cp}})
.
We also verify that in the smooth drift setting,
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}_{2}(\mathsf{KS}_{T})
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
. To see this, we make the observation that

\displaystyle\mathsf{KS}\bigl(\mathrm{Exp}(1),\mathrm{Exp}(1+\varepsilon)\bigr)=\sup_{x\geq 0}\bigl\{\bigl|e^{-x}-e^{-(1+\varepsilon)x}\bigr|\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\geq 0}\bigl\{e^{-x}\bigl(1-e^{-\varepsilon x}\bigr)\bigr\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{x\geq 0}\bigl\{\varepsilon xe^{-x}\bigr\}\leq 2\varepsilon,

where we have used the elementary inequalities
1-e^{-u}\leq u
for
u\geq 0
and
xe^{-x}\leq\frac{x}{1+x}\leq 1
for
x\geq 0
.
Consequently, for any
\{{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{score}}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}\in{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
, it is easily seen that

\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\mathsf{KS}({\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{score}}_{t},{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{score}}_{t+1})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{KS}\bigl(\mathrm{Exp}(1),\mathrm{Exp}(1+\varepsilon)\bigr)\leq 2\varepsilon m.

Choosing
\varepsilon\leq\mathsf{KS}_{T}/(2m)
ensures that
\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\mathsf{KS}({\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{score}}_{t},{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{score}}_{t+1})\leq\mathsf{KS}_{T}
,
and as a result,
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}_{2}(\mathsf{KS}_{T})
.

Step 2: lower bound for a single time segment.

Consider a fixed time segment and suppress the segment index here for notational simplicity. Within this time segment,
the score random variables
\{s_{t}\}
are i.i.d. drawn from either
\mathrm{Exp}(1)
or
\mathrm{Exp}(1+\varepsilon)
. Denote
s_{1:t}=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{t}\}
, let
q_{t}=q(s_{1:t})
be an arbitrary
estimator based on the past observations, and set

q^{\star}_{0}\coloneqq\log(1/\alpha)
and
q^{\star}_{1}\coloneqq\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}\log(1/\alpha).

Denote by
{\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{0,\mathsf{score}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{1,\mathsf{score}}
the joint distributions of
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{t})
when
s_{i}\sim\mathrm{Exp}(1)
and
s_{i}\sim\mathrm{Exp}(1+\varepsilon)
, respectively.
Let the Bernoulli random variable
H\sim\mathrm{Ber}(0.5)
indicate which distribution generates the sequence
\{s_{t}\}
.
Then, letting
s
be a random variable—independent of
s_{1:t}
conditioned on
H
—such that
s\mid H=0\,\sim\mathrm{Exp}(1)
and
s\mid H=1\,\sim\mathrm{Exp}(1+\varepsilon)
, and denoting by
\mathbb{P}_{0}
(resp.
\mathbb{P}_{1}
) the distribution when
H=0
(resp.
H=1
),
we can derive

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{H,s_{1:t}\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{H,\mathsf{score}}}\left[\left|{{\mathbb{P}}_{H}(s>q_{t}\,|\,q_{t})-\alpha}\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}[\left|{{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(s>q_{t}\,|\,q_{t})-\alpha}\right|]+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{1:t}}[\left|{{\mathbb{P}}_{1}(s>q_{t}\,|\,q_{t})-\alpha}\right|]

(55)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\left|{e^{-q_{t}}-\alpha}\right|+\big|e^{-(1+\varepsilon)q_{t}}-\alpha\big|\right)\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\left|{e^{-q_{t}}-\alpha}\right|+\big|e^{-(1+\varepsilon)q_{t}}-\alpha\big|\right)\mathbbm{1}\left\{q_{t}\in{\mathcal{K}}\right\}\right]+\frac{\alpha}{4}{\mathbb{P}}\left(q_{t}\notin{\mathcal{K}}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{H,s_{1:t}\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{H,\mathsf{score}}}\left[\left|{{\mathbb{P}}_{H}(s>q_{t}\,|\,q_{t})-\alpha}\right|\mathbbm{1}\{q_{t}\in{\mathcal{K}}\}\right]+\frac{\alpha}{4}{\mathbb{P}}(q_{t}\notin{\mathcal{K}}),

where we take
{\mathcal{K}}\coloneqq\big[\frac{\log(2/3\alpha)}{1+\varepsilon},\log\frac{2}{\alpha}\big]
. Here, the inequality above holds due to the elementary fact

\left|{e^{-q}-\alpha}\right|+\big|e^{-(1+\varepsilon)q}-\alpha\big|\geq\frac{\alpha}{2}

as long as
q>\log\frac{2}{\alpha}
or
q<\frac{\log(2/3\alpha)}{1+\varepsilon}
.

Furthermore, for any
\lambda\in\{1,1+\varepsilon\}
, the mean value theorem tells us the existence of some
\xi
between
q_{t}
and
q^{\star}_{\lambda}
obeying

|{\mathbb{P}}_{H}(s>q_{t}\,|\,q_{t})-\alpha|=|{\mathbb{P}}_{H}(s>q_{t}\,|\,q_{t})-{\mathbb{P}}_{H}(s>q^{\star}_{H})|=\lambda e^{-\lambda\xi}\,|q_{t}-q^{\star}_{H}|.

Note that when
\varepsilon\leq 1/2
,
1\leq\lambda\leq 1+\varepsilon
and
\xi\leq\log(2/\alpha)
, we have

\lambda e^{-\lambda\xi}\geq e^{-\lambda\log\frac{2}{\alpha}}\geq e^{-(1+\varepsilon)\log\frac{2}{\alpha}}=(\alpha/2)^{1+\varepsilon}\geq\alpha^{1+\varepsilon}/3
, and as a consequence,

|{\mathbb{P}}_{H}(s>q_{t}\,|\,q_{t})-\alpha|\,\mathbbm{1}\{q_{t}\in{\mathcal{K}}\}\;\geq\;\frac{\alpha^{1+\varepsilon}}{3}\,|q_{t}-q^{\star}_{H}|\,\mathbbm{1}\{q_{t}\in{\mathcal{K}}\}.

(56)

Substituting (
56
) into (
55
) leads to

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{H,s_{1:t}\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{H,\mathsf{score}}}\left[\left|{{\mathbb{P}}_{H}(s>q_{t}\,|\,q_{t})-\alpha}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\alpha}{4}{\mathbb{P}}(q_{t}\notin{\mathcal{K}})+{\mathbb{E}}_{H,s_{1:t}\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{H,\mathsf{score}}}\left[\left|{{\mathbb{P}}_{H}(s>q_{t}\,|\,q_{t})-\alpha}\right|\mathbbm{1}\{q_{t}\in{\mathcal{K}}\}\right]

(57)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\alpha^{1+\varepsilon}}{3}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left|{q_{t}-q_{H}^{\star}}\right|\mathbbm{1}\{q_{t}\in{\mathcal{K}}\}\right]+\frac{\alpha}{4}{\mathbb{P}}(q_{t}\notin{\mathcal{K}}).

To further bound the second term on the right-hand side of (
57
), let us look at the following test:

\widehat{H}=0~\text{ if }q_{t}\geq\frac{q^{\star}_{0}+q^{\star}_{1}}{2};\quad\widehat{H}=1~\text{ otherwise}.

Then given that
H\in\{0,1\}
, it can be derived that

\mathbbm{1}\{\widehat{H}\neq H\}\;\leq\;\mathbbm{1}\bigl\{|q_{t}-q^{\star}_{H}|>(q_{0}^{\star}-q_{1}^{\star})/2\bigr\},

and hence

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[|q_{t}-q^{\star}_{H}|\mathbbm{1}\{q_{t}\in{\mathcal{K}}\}\bigr]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{q_{0}^{\star}-q_{1}^{\star}}{2}\,{\mathbb{P}}\Bigl(|q_{t}-q^{\star}_{H}|>\frac{q_{0}^{\star}-q_{1}^{\star}}{2};~q_{t}\in{\mathcal{K}}\Bigr)

(58)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{q_{0}^{\star}-q_{1}^{\star}}{2}\,{\mathbb{P}}(\widehat{H}\neq H;~q_{t}\in{\mathcal{K}}).

In addition, if
q_{0}^{\star}-q_{1}^{\star}=\log\frac{1}{\alpha}-\frac{\log(1/\alpha)}{1+\alpha}=\frac{\varepsilon\log(1/\alpha)}{1+\varepsilon}<\frac{3}{4}
, then we have

\frac{\alpha}{4}{\mathbb{P}}(q_{t}\notin{\mathcal{K}})\geq\frac{\alpha^{1+\varepsilon}}{4}{\mathbb{P}}(q_{t}\notin{\mathcal{K}};\widehat{H}\neq H)\geq\frac{\alpha^{1+\varepsilon}}{3}(q_{0}^{\star}-q_{1}^{\star}){\mathbb{P}}(q_{t}\notin{\mathcal{K}};\widehat{H}\neq H).

(59)

Substituting (
58
) and (
59
) into (
57
) yields

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{H,s_{1:t}\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{H,\mathsf{score}}}\left[\left|{{\mathbb{P}}_{H}(s>q_{t}\,|\,q_{t})-\alpha}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\alpha^{1+\varepsilon}}{6}(q_{0}^{\star}-q_{1}^{\star})\Big({\mathbb{P}}(q_{t}\in{\mathcal{K}};~\widehat{H}\neq H)+{\mathbb{P}}(q_{t}\notin{\mathcal{K}};~\widehat{H}\neq H)\Big)

(60)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{1+\varepsilon}\varepsilon\log(1/\alpha)}{6(1+\varepsilon)}{\mathbb{P}}(\widehat{H}\neq H).

It remains to lower bound the probability
{\mathbb{P}}(\widehat{H}\neq H)
.
Le Cam’s two-point method
(
73
, Theorem 2.2)
and Pinsker’s inequality
(
73
, Lemma 2.5)
imply that:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(\widehat{H}\neq H)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(\widehat{H}\neq 0)+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{P}}_{1}(\widehat{H}\neq 1)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(1-\mathsf{TV}\bigl({\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{0,\mathsf{score}},{\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{1,\mathsf{score}}\bigr)\Bigr)\;\geq\;\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(1-\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathsf{KL}\bigl({\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{0,\mathsf{score}}\,\|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{1,\mathsf{score}}\bigr)}\Bigr).

Since the observations are i.i.d. within this time segment, one has

\mathsf{KL}\bigl({\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{0,\mathsf{score}}\,\|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{1,\mathsf{score}}\bigr)=t\cdot\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{Exp}(1)\,\|\,\mathrm{Exp}(1+\varepsilon)).

Moreover, the KL divergence admits the following closed-form expression:

\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{Exp}(1)\,\|\,\mathrm{Exp}(1+\varepsilon))=\log\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}+(1+\varepsilon)-1=\varepsilon-\log(1+\varepsilon),

and for
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
we have
\varepsilon-\log(1+\varepsilon)\leq\varepsilon^{2}/2
since

\log(1+x)\geq x-x^{2}/2
for
x\in[0,1]
. Therefore,

\mathsf{KL}\bigl({\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{0,\mathsf{score}}\,\|\,{\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{1,\mathsf{score}}\bigr)\leq t\,\varepsilon^{2}/2.

Choosing
t\leq 1/(4\varepsilon^{2})
yields
\mathsf{KL}({\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{0,\mathsf{score}}\|{\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{1,\mathsf{score}})\leq 1/8
, hence

\mathsf{TV}({\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{0,\mathsf{score}},{\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{1,\mathsf{score}})\leq 1/4
. Consequently,

{\mathbb{P}}(\widehat{H}\neq H)=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(\widehat{H}\neq 0)+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{P}}_{1}(\widehat{H}\neq 1)\ \geq\ \frac{3}{8}.

(61)

Substituting (
61
) into (
60
) then yields, for all
\varepsilon\leq 1/(2\sqrt{t})
,

\mathop{{\mathbb{E}}}_{H\sim\mathrm{Ber}(0.5),~s_{1:t}\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{H,\mathsf{score}}}\Bigl[\bigl|{\mathbb{P}}_{H}(s>q_{t}\mid q_{t})-\alpha\bigr|\Bigr]\;\geq\;\frac{\alpha^{1+\varepsilon}\varepsilon\log(1/\alpha)}{6}\cdot\frac{3}{8}=\frac{\alpha^{1+\varepsilon}\varepsilon\log(1/\alpha)}{16}.

(62)

Step 3: extension from one time segment to entire horizon.

We now demonstrate how the single-segment lower bound in Step 2 can be adapted to establish a lower bound for the entire horizon
[T]
.
Recall that, at Step 1,
[T]
is partitioned into
m
consecutive time segments
{\mathcal{I}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{I}}_{m}
obeying
|{\mathcal{I}}_{j}|\asymp T/m
.
Let
H_{1},\dots,H_{m}
be i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables, and construct a random distribution
sequence
\{{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{score}}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
by setting, for each time segment
j
and each
t\in{\mathcal{I}}_{j}
,

{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{score}}_{t}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{Exp}(1),&H_{j}=0,\\
\mathrm{Exp}(1+\varepsilon),&H_{j}=1.\end{cases}

Consider any online procedure producing
q_{t}=q(s_{1:t})
. Condition on the history up to the end of time segment
j-1
.
Since
H_{j}
is independent of the past, the conditional prior on
H_{j}
remains uniform over
\{0,1\}
, and the observations within time segment
j
are i.i.d. from
\mathrm{Exp}(1)
or
\mathrm{Exp}(1+\varepsilon)
accordingly. Therefore, the single-segment
lower bound (
62
) applies for all times within time segment
{\mathcal{I}}_{j}
, with the proviso that
4t\varepsilon^{2}\leq 1
.

To ensure that (
62
) holds throughout the entire time segment
\mathcal{I}_{j}
, we impose the condition

4\varepsilon^{2}\,|{\mathcal{I}}_{j}|\leq 1.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}_{j-1}
denote the
\sigma
-field generated by the samples
{s_{t}}
observed prior to time segment
j
.
We can then take the sum of (
62
) over
t\in{\mathcal{I}}_{j}
to reach, for each
{\mathcal{I}}_{j}
,

{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sum\limits_{t\in{\mathcal{I}}_{j}}\bigl|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{t}>q_{t}\mid q_{t})-\alpha\bigr|\ \bigg|\ {\mathcal{F}}_{j-1}\bigg]\ \geq\ |{\mathcal{I}}_{j}|\cdot\frac{\varepsilon\alpha^{1+\varepsilon}}{16}\cdot\log\frac{1}{\alpha},

and hence, summing over
j=1,\dots,m
and taking expectation over
\{H_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
gives

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{\{{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{score}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{m}}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigg[\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}\bigl|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{t}>q_{t}\mid q_{t})-\alpha\bigr|\Bigg]

\displaystyle\geq\mathop{{\mathbb{E}}}\limits_{H_{1:m},s_{1:T}}\left[\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}\Big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{t}>q_{t}\mid q_{t})-\alpha\Big|\right]

(63)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{{k}={1}}^{m}\sum\limits_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\mathop{{\mathbb{E}}}\limits_{H_{k},s_{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}}\left[\Big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{i}>q_{i}\mid q_{i})-\alpha\Big|\right]

\displaystyle\geq\bigg(\frac{\alpha^{1+\varepsilon}\varepsilon}{16}\log\frac{1}{\alpha}\bigg)\sum\limits_{{k}={1}}^{m}|{\mathcal{I}}_{k}|\geq\frac{T\varepsilon\alpha^{1+\varepsilon}}{16}\log\frac{1}{\alpha},

provided that
\varepsilon\leq\frac{\sqrt{m}}{2\sqrt{T}}
.
To connect this inequality to the advertised minimax lower bounds, we look at the two distribution-shift settings separately.

•

Change-point setting.

In this case,
taking
m=N^{\mathsf{cp}}+1
and
\varepsilon=\sqrt{(N^{\mathsf{cp}}+1)/(4T)}
in
(
63
) yields

\sup_{\{{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{score}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}\bigl|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{t}>q_{t}\mid q_{t})-\alpha\bigr|\right]=\Omega\left(T\varepsilon\,\alpha^{1+\varepsilon}\log\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)=\Omega\left(\alpha^{2}\log(1/\alpha)\sqrt{(N^{\mathsf{cp}}+1)T}\right).

•

Smooth drift setting.

In order for (
63
) to be applicable in this setting, it suffices to choose
\varepsilon
such that

\varepsilon\leq\min\Bigl\{\frac{\mathsf{KS}_{T}}{2m},\ \sqrt{\frac{m}{4T}}\Bigr\}.

Now, if
\mathsf{KS}_{T}\sqrt{T}\geq 1
, then we can choose

m=\mathsf{KS}_{T}^{2/3}T^{1/3}\,(\geq 1),\qquad\varepsilon=\frac{\mathsf{KS}_{T}^{1/3}}{2T^{1/3}},

which satisfies the above requirement. Plugging this choice into (
63
) gives

\displaystyle\sup_{\{{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{score}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}\bigl|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{t}>q_{t}\mid q_{t})-\alpha\bigr|\right]

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(T\varepsilon\,\alpha^{1+\varepsilon}\log\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)=\Omega\left(\alpha^{2}\log(1/\alpha)\,\mathsf{KS}_{T}^{1/3}T^{2/3}\right).

On the other hand, if
\mathsf{KS}_{T}\sqrt{T}<1
, then one can apply (
62
) to the entire horizon
[T]
to arrive at

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{H\sim\mathrm{Ber}(0.5),~s_{1:T}\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{1:T}^{H,\mathsf{score}}}

\displaystyle\bigg[\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}\bigl|{\mathbb{P}}_{H}(s>q_{t}\mid q_{t})-\alpha\bigr|\bigg]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{H\sim\mathrm{Ber}(0.5)}\left[\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{1:t}\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{1:t}^{H,\mathsf{score}}}\left[\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s>q_{t}\mid q_{t})-\alpha\big|\right]\bigg|~H\right]

\displaystyle\;\geq\;\frac{\alpha^{1+\varepsilon}T\varepsilon\log(1/\alpha)}{6}\cdot\frac{3}{8}=\frac{\alpha^{1+\varepsilon}T\varepsilon\log(1/\alpha)}{16},

for all
\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{T}}
.
Thus, taking
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{T}}
leads to

\sup_{\{{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathsf{score}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}\bigl|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{t}>q_{t}\mid q_{t})-\alpha\bigr|\right]=\Omega\left(\alpha^{2}\sqrt{T}\log(1/\alpha)\right).

These two cases taken collectively conclude the proof for the smooth drift setting.

B.3
Proof of auxiliary lemmas

B.3.1
Proof of
Lemma

B.1

Since round
r
has not been terminated due to the detection of distribution shift, it follows that

\left|{\sum\limits_{{l}={i}}^{j}\big(\mathbbm{1}\{s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\}-\alpha\big)}\right|\leq\sigma_{n,r}\sqrt{j-i+1}\leq 24\sqrt{(j-i+1)\log(4\tau_{n,r})}

(64)

for every
1\leq i,j\leq T_{r}
.
On the event
{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}
,
combine the definition (
37
) of
{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}
and Eqn. (
64
) to reach

\left|{\sum\limits_{{l}={i}}^{j}\big({\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big)}\right|\leq 30\sqrt{(j-i+1)\log(4\tau_{n,r})},\quad\text{for all }1\leq i<j\leq T_{r}.

(65)

Below, we look at the two drift settings separately.

Change-point setting.

In this setting, the score distribution remains fixed within each time segment
{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r,k}
.
Consequently, for every
l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r,k}
, the conditional exceedance probability

{\mathbb{P}}\!\left(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r}\right)
is identical (i.e., does not depend on
l
), which in turn implies that

\sum_{l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r,k}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}\!\left(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r}\right)-\alpha\big|=\bigg|\sum_{l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r,k}}\big({\mathbb{P}}\!\left(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r}\right)-\alpha\big)\bigg|.

Combining this with Eqn. (
65
) yields

\bigg(\sum_{l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r,k}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big|\bigg)\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}\}\leq 30\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r,k}}\right|\log(4\tau_{n,r})}.

Summing this inequality over
k=1,\ldots,K_{n,r}
(i.e., summing over all segments in this round) yields the advertised bound for the change-point setting.

Smooth drift setting.

Consider a given
q_{n,r}
. Partition the time indices
1,2,\ldots,T_{r}
into
K
consecutive time segments
{\mathcal{I}}_{k}\coloneqq\{i_{k-1}+1,\ldots,i_{k}\}
,
k=1,\ldots,K
, with
i_{0}=0
and
i_{K}=T_{r}
. This partition is chosen so that, for
l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{1},{\mathcal{I}}_{3},\ldots,{\mathcal{I}}_{2[\frac{K-1}{2}]+1}
, the quantity
{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r})-\alpha
has the same sign; without loss of generality, assume that the signs are positive. Then for any
l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{2}\cup\cdots\cup{\mathcal{I}}_{2[\frac{K}{2}]}
, we have
{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r})-\alpha<0
. Consequently, the cumulative regret within this round can be expressed by grouping terms with positive and negative signs as follows:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r}}

\displaystyle\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big|

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k:k\text{ is odd}}\sum\limits_{l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\big({\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big)+\sum\limits_{k:k\text{ is even}}\sum\limits_{l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\big(\alpha-{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})\big)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{{k}={1}}^{K}\sum\limits_{l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\big({\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big)+2\sum\limits_{k:k\text{ is even}}\sum\limits_{l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\big(\alpha-{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})\big).

(66)

The first term on the right-hand side of (
66
) can be readily controlled
on the event
{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}
; more specifically, it is seen from (
65
) that, on the event
{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}
,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{k}={1}}^{K}\sum\limits_{l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\big({\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big)=\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r}}\big({\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big)\leq 30\sqrt{T_{r}\log(4\tau_{n,r})}.

(67)

We then turn to the second term on the right-hand side of (
66
). For every
k\in[K]
, set

A_{k}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|}\sum_{l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\big(\alpha-{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})\big).

On the event
{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}
, it agains follows from (
65
) that

\frac{\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|}}{30\sqrt{\log(4\tau_{n,r})}}A_{k}=\frac{\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|}}{30\sqrt{\log(4\tau_{n,r})}}\left(\frac{1}{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|}\sum_{l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\big(\alpha-{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r})\mid q_{n,r}\big)\right)\leq 1.

(68)

This allows one to deduce that, on the event
{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}
,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k:k\text{ is even}}\sum\limits_{l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}

\displaystyle\big(\alpha-{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})\big)=\sum\limits_{k:k\text{ is even}}\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|\cdot A_{k}=\sum\limits_{k:k\text{ is even}}\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|^{\frac{2}{3}}\left(\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|^{\frac{1}{2}}A_{k}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}A_{k}^{\frac{1}{3}}

\displaystyle{\overset{\eqref{eq:one_round_regret_3}}{\leq}}\sum\limits_{k:k\text{ is even}}30\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|^{\frac{2}{3}}A_{k}^{\frac{1}{3}}\sqrt{\log T}\leq 30\sqrt{\log T}\bigg(\sum\limits_{k:k\text{ is even}}\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|\bigg)^{\frac{2}{3}}\bigg(\sum\limits_{k:k\text{ is even}}A_{k}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{3}},

(69)

where the last inequality follows from Hölder’s inequality.
This leaves us with two sums to control.

•

Regarding the summation of
|\mathcal{I}_{k}|
, it is easily seen that

\sum\limits_{k:k\text{ is even}}\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|\leq\sum\limits_{{k}={1}}^{K}\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|=T_{r}.

(70)

•

Let us now turn to the summation of
A_{k}
. Given the way we partition the sets
{\mathcal{I}}_{k}
, we see that for any even number
k~(\geq 2)
,
{\mathbb{P}}\big(s_{n,r,i_{k-1}}>q_{n,r}\big)>\alpha
.
Consequently, for any even
k
, one can bound
A_{k}
as

\displaystyle A_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|}\sum_{l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\big(\alpha-{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})\big)

(71)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|}\sum_{l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\big({\mathbb{P}}\big(s_{n,r,i_{k-1}}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r}\big)-{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})\big)=\frac{1}{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|}\sum\limits_{l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\sum\limits_{{i}={i_{k-1}}}^{l-1}\Delta_{n,r,i},

where we define

\Delta_{n,r,i}\coloneqq{\mathbb{P}}\big(s_{n,r,i}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r}\big)-{\mathbb{P}}\big(s_{n,r,i+1}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r}\big).

The definition (
6
) of the KS distance tells us that

\Delta_{n,r,i}\leq\mathsf{KS}\big(s_{n,r,i},s_{n,r,i+1}\big)\quad\text{for all }i\in[T_{r}],

which combined with Eqn. (
71
) leads to

\displaystyle A_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|}\sum\limits_{l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\sum\limits_{{i}={i_{k-1}}}^{l-1}\mathsf{KS}\big(s_{n,r,i},s_{n,r,i+1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|}\sum\limits_{l\in{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\sum\limits_{{i}={i_{k-1}}}^{i_{k}-1}\mathsf{KS}\big(s_{n,r,i},s_{n,r,i+1}\big)=\sum\limits_{{i}={i_{k-1}}}^{i_{k}-1}\mathsf{KS}\big(s_{n,r,i},s_{n,r,i+1}\big).

(72)

Let
s_{n,r,0}=s_{n,r,1}
. Summing over all even
k
yields

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k:k\text{ is even}}A_{k}\leq\sum\limits_{{k}={1}}^{K}\sum\limits_{{i}={i_{k-1}}}^{i_{k}-1}\mathsf{KS}\big(s_{n,r,i},s_{n,r,i+1}\big)=\sum\limits_{{i}={0}}^{T_{r}}\mathsf{KS}\big(s_{n,r,i},s_{n,r,i+1}\big).

(73)

Putting Eqns. (
70
) and (
73
) together yields

\left(\sum\limits_{k:k\text{ is even}}\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}}\right|\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}\left(\sum\limits_{k:k\text{ is even}}A_{k}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\leq T_{r}^{\frac{2}{3}}\left(\sum\limits_{{i}={1}}^{T_{r}}\mathsf{KS}\big(s_{n,r,i},s_{n,r,i+1}\big)\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}=\big(\mathsf{KS}_{n,r}^{\mathsf{round}}\big)^{\frac{1}{3}}T_{r}^{\frac{2}{3}},

which taken together with Eqns. (
66
), (
67
) and (
69
) establishes that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r}}\Big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\Big|\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}\}

\displaystyle\leq 30\sqrt{\log T}\left(\sqrt{T_{r}}+\big(\mathsf{KS}_{n,r}^{\mathsf{round}}\big)^{\frac{1}{3}}T_{r}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right)=\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{T_{r}}+\big(\mathsf{KS}_{n,r}^{\mathsf{round}}\big)^{\frac{1}{3}}T_{r}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right).

B.3.2
Proof of
Lemma

B.2

Consider stage
n
, and
denote by
t_{n}
the number of iterations in the
r_{n}
-th round (recall that
r_{n}
denotes the index of the last round of stage
n
). Observe that for any
r\in[r_{n}-1]
(resp. for
r=r_{n}
), no initiation of a new stage—i.e., no detection of distribution drift—is triggered within iterations
\{1,\ldots,T_{r}\}
(resp.
\{1,\ldots,t_{n}-1\}
). In what follows, we look at the two drift settings separately.

Change-point setting.

Note that the collection of time segments
\{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r,k}\}
described in
Section

B.1.1
can be viewed as a refinement of

\{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,j}\}_{j=1}^{J_{n}}
; for instance, a given
\mathcal{I}_{n,j}
might appear in more than one round, possibly due to imperfect drift detection.
For each
j\in[J_{n}]
, denote by

r^{(j)}
the index of the first round that overlaps with the segment
{\mathcal{I}}_{n,j}
; it is straightforward to see that the last round that overlaps with
{\mathcal{I}}_{n,j}
cannot exceed
r^{(j+1)}
(here, if this is already the last time segment in stage
n
, we can simply let
r^{(j+1)}
be the last round of this stage). Note that

{\mathcal{I}}_{n,j}
may share its first and/or last
round with
{\mathcal{I}}_{n,j-1}
or
{\mathcal{I}}_{n,j+1}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{n,j}\subseteq\Bigg\{\bigcup\limits_{r=r^{(j)}+1}^{r^{(j+1)}-1}\bigcup\limits_{k=1}^{K_{n,r}}{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r,k}\Bigg\}\cup\left\{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r^{(j)},K_{n,r^{(j)}}}\right\}\cup\left\{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r^{(j+1)},1}\right\},

(74)

which in turn gives

\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}}\sum\limits_{{k}={1}}^{K_{n,r}}\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r,k}}\right|}\leq\sum\limits_{{j}={1}}^{J_{n}}\left\{\sum\limits_{{r}={r^{(j)}+1}}^{r^{(j+1)}-1}\sum\limits_{{k}={1}}^{K_{n,r}}\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r,k}}\right|}+\sqrt{\big|{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r^{(j)},K_{n,r^{(j)}}}\big|}+\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r^{(j+1)},1}}\right|}\right\}.

(75)

Recognizing that each of the intermediate rounds
r^{(j)}+1,\ldots,r^{(j+1)}-1
is fully contained within
\mathcal{I}_{n,j}
, we see that, by construction, each of these rounds also contains a single time segment from the collection
\{\mathcal{I}_{n,r,k}\}
.
This means that for each
r\in\{r^{(j)}+1,\ldots,r^{(j+1)}-1\}
, we have

\sum\limits_{{k}={1}}^{K_{n,r}}\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r,k}}\right|}=\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r,1}}\right|}\leq\sqrt{T_{r}}.

Consequently, for
each
j\in[J_{n}]
, we can bound

\sum\limits_{{r}={r^{(j)}+1}}^{r^{(j+1)-1}}\sum\limits_{{k}={1}}^{K_{n,r}}\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r,k}}\right|}+\sqrt{\big|{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r^{(j)},K_{n,r^{(j)}}}\big|}+\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r^{(j+1)},1}}\right|}{\leq}\sum\limits_{{r}={r^{(j)}+1}}^{r^{(j+1)-1}}\sqrt{T_{r}}+2\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,j}}\right|},

(76)

where the last inequality holds since
{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r^{(j)},K_{n,r^{(j)}}}\cup{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r^{(j+1)},1}\subseteq{\mathcal{I}}_{n,j}
.

Next, we bound the summation of
\sqrt{T_{r}}
on the right-hand side of (
76
).
If
r^{(j)}+1>r^{(j+1)}-1
, then this summation term is equal to
0
; otherwise, it can be seen that (by construction)

T_{r^{(j+1)}-1}=\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r^{(j+1)}-1,1}}\right|\leq\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,j}}\right|,

which implies that

\sum\limits_{{r}={r^{(j)}+1}}^{r^{(j+1)-1}}\sqrt{T_{r}}\leq\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r^{(j+1)-1}}\sqrt{T_{r}}\overset{\mathrm{(a)}}{=}\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r^{(j+1)-1}}3^{\frac{r}{2}}\leq 3\cdot 3^{\frac{r^{(j+1)}-1}{2}}\overset{\mathrm{(b)}}{=}3\sqrt{T_{r^{(j+1)}-1}}\leq 3\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,j}}\right|}.

(77)

Here, (a) and (b) are valid due to our choice
T_{r}=3^{r}
and the fact that, except for the last round of this stage, the
r
-th round has time length exactly equal to
T_{r}
.

Invoking
Lemma

B.1
as well as (
39
), we can demonstrate that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}}\bigg\{\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big|\bigg\}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}-1}\bigg\{\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big|\bigg\}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}\}+\bigg\{\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{t_{n}-1}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-\alpha\big|\bigg\}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r_{n}}\}+1

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{O}\bigg(\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}}\sum\limits_{{k}={1}}^{K_{n,r}}\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r,k}}\right|}\bigg)+1\leq\widetilde{O}\bigg(\sum\limits_{{j}={1}}^{J_{n}}\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,j}}\right|}\bigg),

where the last line follows from
Lemma

B.1
and the fact that no distribution shift has been detected before the last iteration of stage
n
. This taken collectively with (
75
)-(
77
) then yields

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}}\bigg\{\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big|\bigg\}\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}\}\leq\widetilde{O}\bigg(\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}}\sum\limits_{{k}={1}}^{K_{n,r}}\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,r,k}}\right|}\bigg)\leq\widetilde{O}\bigg(\sum\limits_{{j}={1}}^{J_{n}}\sqrt{\left|{{\mathcal{I}}_{n,j}}\right|}\bigg)

as claimed.

Smooth drift setting.

To begin with, Lemma
B.1
tells us that

\displaystyle\bigg\{\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big|\mathbbm{1}\bigg\}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}\}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{T_{r}}+\big(\mathsf{KS}_{n,r}^{\mathsf{round}}\big)^{\frac{1}{3}}T_{r}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right),\quad r=1,2,\ldots,r_{n}-1

(78a)

and

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{t_{n}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-\alpha\big|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r_{n}}\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{t_{n}-1}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-\alpha\big|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r_{n}}\}+1

\displaystyle=\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{t_{n}}+\big(\mathsf{KS}_{n,r_{n}}^{\mathsf{round}}\big)^{\frac{1}{3}}t_{n}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right).

(78b)

Sum Eqn. (
78
) over all rounds in this stage to arrive at:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}-1}\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{T_{r}}

\displaystyle\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r,l}>q_{n,r}\mid q_{n,r})-\alpha\big|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r}\}+\sum\limits_{{l}={1}}^{t_{n}}\big|{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-\alpha\big|\mathbbm{1}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r_{n}}\}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}-1}\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{T_{r}}+\big(\mathsf{KS}_{n,r}^{\mathsf{round}}\big)^{\frac{1}{3}}T_{r}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right)+\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{t_{n}}+\big(\mathsf{KS}_{n,r_{n}}^{\mathsf{round}}\big)^{\frac{1}{3}}t_{n}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(a)}}{\leq}\widetilde{O}\left(\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}}3^{\frac{r}{2}}+\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}-1}\big(\mathsf{KS}_{n,r}^{\mathsf{round}}\big)^{\frac{1}{3}}T_{r}^{\frac{2}{3}}+\big(\mathsf{KS}_{n,r_{n}}^{\mathsf{round}}\big)^{\frac{1}{3}}t_{n}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(b)}}{\leq}\widetilde{O}\left(3^{\frac{r_{n}}{2}}+\bigg(\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}}\big(\mathsf{KS}_{n,r}^{\mathsf{round}}\big)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{3}}S_{n}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right)\overset{\mathrm{(c)}}{\leq}\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{S_{n}}+\mathsf{KS}_{n,S_{n}}^{\frac{1}{3}}S_{n}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right).

Here, (a) holds since
T_{r}=3^{r}
and
t_{n}\leq 3^{r_{n}}
, (b) follows from Hölder’s inequality, and (c) holds since the total number
S_{n}
of time points within stage
n
satisfies

\sqrt{S_{n}}\geq\bigg(\sum\limits_{{r}={1}}^{r_{n}-1}3^{r}\bigg)^{1/2}\asymp 3^{\frac{r_{n}}{2}}.

This taken together with (
39
) concludes the proof.

B.3.3
Proof of
Lemma

B.3

We establish this lemma by contradiction. Suppose that round
r_{n}-1
and round
r_{n}
are completely contained within the same time segment from the collection
\{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N^{\mathsf{cp}}+1}
. According to the procedure of
DriftOCP
, on the event
{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r_{n}-1}\cap{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r_{n}}
there exists an index

j_{n}
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T_{r_{n}-1}}\Bigg|\sum_{l=1}^{T_{r_{n}-1}}\Bigl(\mathbbm{1}\{s_{n,r_{n}-1,l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\}-\alpha\Bigr)\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T_{r_{n}-1}},

(79a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t_{n}-j_{n}+1}\Bigg|\sum_{l=j_{n}}^{t_{n}}\Bigl(\mathbbm{1}\{s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\}-\alpha\Bigr)\Bigg|

\displaystyle>\frac{24\sqrt{\log(4\tau_{n,r_{n}})}}{\sqrt{t_{n}-j_{n}+1}},

(79b)

where (
79a
) is valid since
q_{n,r_{n}}
is taken to be the
\alpha
-empirical-quantile of the set
\{s_{n,r_{n}-1,l}\}_{l=1}^{T_{r_{n}-1}}
, and in (
79b
) we use the detection threshold
\sigma_{n,r}=24\sqrt{\log(4\tau_{n,r})}
.

Since the two rounds lie within the same time
segment from the collection
\{{\mathcal{I}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N^{\mathsf{cp}}+1}
, the scores in these two rounds are identically distributed.
Let
s
denote an independent copy of the score from this segment. Then, on

{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r_{n}-1}\cap{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r_{n}}
(cf. (
37
)), we can invoke (
79a
) and the triangle inequality to obtain

\displaystyle\Bigl|{\mathbb{P}}(s>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-\alpha\Bigr|=\frac{1}{T_{r_{n}-1}}\Bigg|\sum_{l=1}^{T_{r_{n}-1}}\Bigl(\mathbb{P}(s_{n,r_{n}-1,l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-\alpha\Bigr)\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T_{r_{n}-1}}\Bigg|\sum_{l=1}^{T_{r_{n}-1}}\Bigl(\mathbbm{1}\{s>q_{n,r_{n}}\}-\alpha\Bigr)\Bigg|+\frac{1}{T_{r_{n}-1}}\Bigg|\sum_{l=1}^{T_{r_{n}-1}}\Bigl(\mathbbm{1}\{s_{n,r_{n}-1,l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\}-\mathbb{P}(s>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})\Bigr)\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T_{r_{n}-1}}+\frac{6\sqrt{\log\tau_{n+1}}}{\sqrt{T_{r_{n}-1}}}\leq\frac{7\sqrt{\log\tau_{n+1}}}{\sqrt{T_{r_{n-1}}}}\leq\frac{14\sqrt{\log\tau_{n+1}}}{\sqrt{T_{r_{n}}}}.

(80)

In the meantime, applying (
79b
) again on the same event and invoking the triangle inequality gives

\displaystyle\Bigl|{\mathbb{P}}(s>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-\alpha\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t_{n}-j_{n}+1}\left|{\sum\limits_{{l}={j_{n}}}^{t_{n}}\Bigl({\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})-\alpha\Bigr)}\right|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{t_{n}-j_{n}+1}\Bigg|\sum_{l=j_{n}}^{t_{n}}\Bigl(\mathbbm{1}\{s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\}-\alpha\Bigr)\Bigg|

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{t_{n}-j_{n}+1}\Bigg|\sum_{l=j_{n}}^{t_{n}}\Bigl(\mathbbm{1}\{s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\}-{\mathbb{P}}(s_{n,r_{n},l}>q_{n,r_{n}}\mid q_{n,r_{n}})\Bigr)\Bigg|

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(a)}}{\geq}\frac{24\sqrt{\log(4\tau_{n,r_{n}})}}{\sqrt{t_{n}-j_{n}+1}}-\frac{6\sqrt{\log\tau_{n+1}}}{\sqrt{t_{n}-j_{n}+1}}\geq\frac{18\sqrt{\log\tau_{n+1}}}{\sqrt{T_{r_{n}}}},

(81)

where (a) follows by combining (
79b
) with the event
{\mathcal{A}}_{n,r_{n}}
.
However, (
81
) contradicts (
B.3.3
), which in turn completes the proof.

Appendix C
Detailed proofs in
Section

4

This section is devoted to establishing the main results in
Section

4
.
Throughout this section, we define,
for any cumulative distribution function (CDF)
F
, the quantile function

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}_{1-\alpha}(F)\coloneqq\inf\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:F(x)\geq 1-\alpha\}.

(82)

Also, we denote by
{\mathcal{C}}(\cdot\mid{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathsf{cal}},{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathsf{train}})
the prediction-set mapping constructed via (
22
), where
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathsf{train}}
is used to fit the model and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathsf{cal}}
is used to form the quantile.

C.1
Proof of Proposition
4.1

We first present the proof of Proposition
4.1
, which concerns the training-conditional coverage guarantees for standard full conformal methods.
Before embarking on the proof, we introduce the following convenient notation (when there is no ambiguity), which shall be used repeatedly throughout Section
C.1
.

Definition C.1

(Basic notation)
.

We introduce the following notation, all conditioned on the realization
Z_{m+1:n}^{\mathsf{train}}=z_{m+1:n}^{\mathsf{train}}
(i.e., the portion of the training set that is disjoint from the calibration set).

•

For any dataset
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathbb{R}}
, let

\widehat{\mu}_{{\mathcal{S}}}(\cdot)\coloneqq{\mathcal{A}}\big({\mathcal{S}}\cup z_{m+1:n}^{\mathsf{train}}\big)
be the fitted model trained obtained by algorithm
\mathcal{A}
on
\mathcal{S}\cup z_{m+1:n}^{\mathsf{train}}
.

•

For any dataset
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathbb{R}}
and any
Z=(X,Y)
, let

\widehat{\mu}^{Z}_{{\mathcal{S}}}(\cdot)\coloneqq\widehat{\mu}_{{\mathcal{S}}\cup\{Z\}}(\cdot)
be the fitted model trained obtained by algorithm
\mathcal{A}
on
\mathcal{S}\cup\{(X,Y)\}\cup z_{m+1:n}^{\mathsf{train}}
.

•

For any dataset
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathbb{R}}
and any
Z=(X,Y)
,
Z^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
, define the scores

s_{{\mathcal{S}}}(Z^{\prime})\coloneqq\bigl|Y^{\prime}-\widehat{\mu}_{{\mathcal{S}}}(X^{\prime})\bigr|\qquad\text{and}\qquad s^{Z}_{{\mathcal{S}}}(Z^{\prime})\coloneqq\bigl|Y^{\prime}-\widehat{\mu}^{Z}_{{\mathcal{S}}}(X^{\prime})\bigr|.

(83)

Remark C.1
