L(\vartheta)\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{{i}={1}}^{n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{Z_{i}\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{i}}\big[\ell(\vartheta;Z_{i})\big].

Note that we allow for a non-identically distributed sequence
\{Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, which is compatible
with the drifting environments considered in the present paper.

We impose the following standard assumptions ensuring well-posedness and curvature of the loss functions.

Assumption D.1
.

Suppose that the loss functions satisfy the following properties:

1.

L
is
\mu
-strongly convex on
{\mathcal{C}}
for some
\mu>0
, in the sense that

\nabla^{2}L(\vartheta)\succeq\mu I_{d}\qquad\text{for all }\vartheta\in{\mathcal{C}}.

2.

For any
\vartheta\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
\left\|{\vartheta}\right\|_{2}\leq D_{\mathcal{C}}
.

3.

For any
\vartheta,\vartheta^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
Z
,
\left\|{\nabla_{\vartheta}\ell(\vartheta;Z)}\right\|\leq\beta_{L}
and
\left\|{\nabla_{\vartheta}^{2}\ell(\vartheta;Z)-\nabla_{\vartheta}^{2}\ell(\vartheta^{\prime};Z)}\right\|_{\infty,\infty}\leq\beta_{s}\left\|{\vartheta-\vartheta^{\prime}}\right\|_{2}
. Here
\left\|{A}\right\|_{\infty,\infty}\coloneqq\max\limits_{i,j\in[d]}\left|{A_{ij}}\right|
.

With Assumption
D.1
in place, we obtain the following stability guarantees for the
constrained M-estimator.

Proposition D.1
.

Suppose that Assumption
D.1
holds. For any
n\geq\frac{32d\beta_{s}^{2}D_{\mathcal{C}}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\log\big(\frac{12\beta_{s}D_{\mathcal{C}}}{\mu}\big)
, We can find a typical set
{\mathcal{E}}
in the
n
-sample
space
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{Z}\times n}
obeying
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}}^{c})\leq d^{2}\exp\big\{-\frac{\mu^{2}n}{32\beta_{s}^{2}D_{\mathcal{C}}^{2}}\big\}
such that the following holds:
consider two adjacent datasets in
{\mathcal{E}}
,
\{Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{Z_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, that differ only in the last
coordinate, i.e.,
Z_{i}^{\prime}=Z_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n-1
and
Z_{n}^{\prime}\neq Z_{n}
,
and denote by
\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}
and
\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime}
the corresponding constrained M-estimates

\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}\coloneqq\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\vartheta\in{\mathcal{C}}}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(\vartheta;Z_{i})\right\},\quad\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime}=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\vartheta\in{\mathcal{C}}}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\ell(\vartheta;Z_{i})+\ell(\vartheta;Z_{n}^{\prime})\right\},

then one has

\big\|\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}-\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime}\big\|_{2}\leq\frac{4\beta_{L}}{\mu n}.

The proof of Proposition
D.1
is deferred to
Section

D.4.1
. With the above proposition in hand, consider a prediction model
\mu(\cdot\mid\vartheta)
. Assume that, for every input
x
, the mapping
\vartheta\rightarrow\mu(x\mid\vartheta)
is
L_{0}
-Lipschitz. Then, by
Proposition

D.1
, for any two neighboring data sequences
\{Z_{n}\}
and
\{Z_{n}^{\prime}\}
that both lie in
{\mathcal{E}}
(which happens with high probability), the corresponding fitted models
\mu(\cdot\mid\widehat{\vartheta})
and
\mu(\cdot\mid\widehat{\vartheta}^{\prime})
satisfy:

\big|\mu(x\mid\widehat{\vartheta})-\mu(x\mid\widehat{\vartheta}^{\prime})\big|\leq L_{0}\big\|\widehat{\vartheta}-\widehat{\vartheta}^{\prime}\big\|_{2}\leq\frac{4\beta_{L}L_{0}}{\mu n},\quad\forall x\in{\mathcal{X}},

thereby validating Assumption
4.3
for this setting with
L_{2}=\frac{4\beta_{L}L_{0}}{\mu}
.

D.2
Linear stochastic approximation

Next, we verify stability for an important class of
online
learning methods based on stochastic approximation.
Unlike the previous example in
Section

D.1
, where the estimator is obtained via empirical risk minimization,
stochastic approximation updates the parameter incrementally as new data arrive
(
21
)
. This makes it particularly
well-suited to our online conformal framework, as it avoids retraining from scratch while still enabling control over the sensitivity of the fitted model to individual observations.

To be concrete, consider a linear prediction model

\widehat{\mu}(x\mid Z_{1:n})=x^{\top}\vartheta_{n},

where the parameter
\vartheta_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is updated online using the data
Z_{1:n}=\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
. At iteration

n
, the squared loss function is defined as

\ell_{n}(\vartheta)=(Y_{n}-X_{n}^{\top}\vartheta)^{2}=\vartheta^{\top}X_{n}X_{n}^{\top}\vartheta-2Y_{n}X_{n}^{\top}\vartheta+Y_{n}^{2}.

Its gradient at
\vartheta_{n}
is given by

\nabla\ell_{n}(\vartheta_{n})=\underset{\eqqcolon\,\widehat{A}_{n}}{\underbrace{2X_{n}X_{n}^{\top}}}\vartheta_{n}-\underset{\eqqcolon\,\widehat{b}_{n}}{\underbrace{2Y_{n}X_{n}}}=\widehat{A}_{n}\vartheta_{n}-\widehat{b}_{n}.

The linear stochastic approximation (LSA) recursion with a decaying stepsize
\eta_{n}
for iteration
n
is

\displaystyle\vartheta_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{n}-\eta_{n}\bigl(\widehat{A}_{n}\vartheta_{n}-\widehat{b}_{n}\bigr)=\bigl(I-\eta_{n}\widehat{A}_{n}\bigr)\vartheta_{n}+\eta_{n}\widehat{b}_{n}=\bigl(I-\eta_{n}A_{n}-\eta_{n}\widetilde{A}_{n}\bigr)\vartheta_{n}+\eta_{n}\widehat{b}_{n},

(220)

where we denote
A_{n}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[\widehat{A}_{n}]
and
\widetilde{A}_{n}\coloneqq\widehat{A}_{n}-A_{n}
.

To validate stability, we compare the LSA iterates generated from two adjacent data streams. Consider two sequences
\{(\widehat{A}_{i},\widehat{b}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{(\widehat{A}_{i}^{\prime},\widehat{b}_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i=1}^{n}
that differ
in exactly one index
l
, i.e.,

(\widehat{A}_{i},\widehat{b}_{i})=(\widehat{A}_{i}^{\prime},\widehat{b}_{i}^{\prime})
for all
i\neq l
, but

(\widehat{A}_{l},\widehat{b}_{l})\neq(\widehat{A}_{l}^{\prime},\widehat{b}_{l}^{\prime})
.
We impose the following assumptions.

Assumption D.2
.

Suppose that there exist constants
L,\mu>0
and
\widehat{\sigma},\sigma\geq 1
satisfying the following properties:

1.

A_{i}\succeq\mu I
for all
i\geq 1
;

2.

\|\widehat{A}_{i}\|\leq\widehat{\sigma},~\|\widetilde{A}_{i}\|\leq\sigma,~\|\widehat{b}_{i}\|_{2}\leq L
for all
i\geq 1
.

With Assumption
D.2
in place, we establish stability of the terminal LSA
iterate. In particular, changing a single observation in the data stream alters
\vartheta_{n+1}
by at most

O((\log^{3}n)/n)
with high probability.

Proposition D.2

(Bounded differences for LSA)
.

Consider any fixed
\zeta\geq 1
, and suppose that
Assumption
D.2
holds.
Assume that the LSA recursion (
220
) adopts the stepsize
\eta_{n}=\min\{1/\widehat{\sigma},\gamma_{n}/n\}
at iteration
n
, where
\gamma_{n}=C\log n
for some constant
C\geq\frac{2(\zeta+1)}{\mu}>0
.
Then there exists a constant
K>0
(independent of
n
) such that, for any
n\geq d^{\frac{1}{\zeta}}
and any two adjacent datasets differing in a
single time index
l
, the corresponding LSA iterates
\{\vartheta_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
\{\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
satisfy

\big\|\vartheta_{n+1}-\vartheta_{n+1}^{\prime}\big\|_{2}\leq K\frac{\log^{3}n}{n}

with probability at least
1-n^{-\zeta}
.

The proof of Proposition
D.2
is postponed to
Section

D.4.2
.
We now return to verify Assumption
4.3
. In this example, our prediction model takes the form

\widehat{\mu}(x\mid Z_{1:n})=x^{\top}\vartheta_{n}.

Assume that the covariate
X
is essentially bounded, i.e.,
\left\|{X}\right\|\leq B_{x}
almost surely. Then, for any two neighboring samples
Z_{1:n}
and
Z_{1:n}^{\prime}
in a typical set
{\mathcal{E}}
with
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}})\geq 1-n^{-\zeta}
, the corresponding parameter estimates—denoted by
\vartheta_{n}
and
\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}
, respectively—obtained by LSA satisfy

\big|~x^{\top}\vartheta_{n}-x^{\top}\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}\big|\leq\left\|{x}\right\|_{2}\left\|{\vartheta_{n}-\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}}\right\|_{2}\leq B_{x}K\frac{\log^{3}n}{n}.

Hence, this justifies Assumption
4.3
with
L_{2}=B_{x}K\log^{3}(m+1)
(with
m
the size of the training set used in this assumption).

D.3
Stochastic strongly convex optimization

We now turn to another case where the predictive model
\widehat{\mu}(\cdot\mid\{Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})
is trained in an adaptive manner.
For parametric statistical models, a natural approach is to maintain a parameter vector and update it using an incremental
optimization rule. Suppose that the model used at iteration
\tau
is

\widehat{\mu}_{\tau}(\cdot)=\widehat{\mu}(\cdot\mid\vartheta_{\tau})
, where
\vartheta_{\tau}
is learned from the
data
\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{\tau-1}
via stochastic optimization (or, more generally, an iterative online training
procedure). Starting from
\vartheta_{\tau-1}
, after observing the new data point
(X_{\tau-1},Y_{\tau-1})
we
update

\vartheta_{\tau}=\vartheta_{\tau-1}-\eta_{\tau-1}f\big(\vartheta_{\tau-1};(X_{\tau-1},Y_{\tau-1})\big),

(221)

where
f(\vartheta;(X,Y))
denotes the update direction and
\eta_{\tau-1}
the stepsize. Let
\Theta
denote the parameter domain and
\mathcal{Z}
the domain of data points.

To study stability for such adaptive methods, we impose several standard conditions on the update map
f
.

Assumption D.3
.

There exist constants
0<\mu\leq L
and
B>0
such that, for any
\vartheta,\vartheta^{\prime}\in\Theta
and

(x,y)\in{\mathcal{Z}}
,

•

Strong convexity:
\left\langle f(\vartheta;(x,y))-f(\vartheta^{\prime};(x,y)),\vartheta-\vartheta^{\prime}\right\rangle\geq\mu\|\vartheta-\vartheta^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}
;

•

Smoothness:
\|f(\vartheta;(x,y))-f(\vartheta^{\prime};(x,y))\|_{2}\leq L\|\vartheta-\vartheta^{\prime}\|_{2}
;

•

Boundedness:
\left\|{f(\vartheta;(x,y))}\right\|_{2}\leq B
.

With Assumption
D.3
in place and suitably decaying stepsizes, a single-sample
perturbation has an
O(1/n)
effect on the parameter iterate, as asserted in the following lemma.
The proof is provided in
Section

D.4.3
.

Proposition D.3
.

Suppose that Assumption
D.3
holds. Consider the parameter sequence

\{\vartheta_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
updated according to (
221
) with stepsize

\eta_{n}=\min\{\gamma/n,\,1/L\}
, where
\gamma>3/\mu
. Then there exists a constant
K>0
(independent of
n
)
such that, for any two adjacent datasets differing in a single time index
l
, the corresponding iterates

\{\vartheta_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
\{\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
satisfy

\big\|\vartheta_{n}-\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}\big\|_{2}\leq\frac{K}{n}.

We now discuss how to incorporate the above adaptive updates into the online full conformal construction. In particular, when forming the
augmented dataset
(X_{n,r,l},y)
in the proposed full conformal procedure, we update the model parameter using the same one-step
rule. For any pair
(x,y)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}
, define

\vartheta_{\tau}^{(x,y)}\coloneqq\vartheta_{\tau-1}-\eta_{\tau-1}f\big(\vartheta_{\tau-1};(x,y)\big).

Accordingly, in round
r
of stage
n
, we redefine the residual score
s_{i}(X_{n,r,l},y)
from Eqn. (
26
) as follows:

\displaystyle s_{i}^{(X,y)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Big|Y_{n,r-1,i}-\widehat{\mu}\big(X_{n,r-1,i}^{\mathsf{cal}}\,|\,\vartheta_{\tau_{n,r}}^{(X,y)}\big)\Big|,\qquad i=1,\ldots,T_{r-1},

(222a)

\displaystyle s_{\mathsf{test}}^{(X,y)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Big|y-\widehat{\mu}\big(X\,|\,\vartheta_{\tau_{n,r}}^{(X,y)}\big)\Big|.

(222b)

Equipped with (
222
), we can then construct the prediction set according to (
27
). This leads to a modification of
Algorithm
4

that incorporates adaptive updates of the fitted model.

D.4
Detailed proofs

D.4.1
Proof of Propostion
D.1

Since
{\mathcal{C}}
is convex and
\ell(\cdot;z)
is differentiable in the first argument, the standard optimality condition
(
23
)
yields

\displaystyle\big\langle\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}),\vartheta-\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\geq 0\quad\text{for all }\vartheta\in{\mathcal{C}},

(223)

\displaystyle\big\langle\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}^{\prime}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime}),\vartheta-\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\geq 0\quad\text{for all }\vartheta\in{\mathcal{C}}.

(224)

In particular, taking
\vartheta=\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime}
in (
223
) and
\vartheta=\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}
in (
224
), adding these two inequalities, and setting
\Delta_{n}\coloneqq\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime}-\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}
, we obtain

\big\langle\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n})-\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}^{\prime}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime}),\Delta_{n}\big\rangle\geq 0.

(225)

By construction, the gradients of the two empirical loss functions satisfy

\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}^{\prime}(\vartheta)=\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\vartheta)+\frac{1}{n}\big(\nabla\ell(\vartheta;Z_{n}^{\prime})-\nabla\ell(\vartheta;Z_{n})\big),

which, when evaluated at
\vartheta=\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime}
, yields

\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}^{\prime}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime})=\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{n}\big(\nabla\ell(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime},Z_{n}^{\prime})-\nabla\ell(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime},Z_{n})\big).

Substituting this identity into (
225
) yields

\big\langle\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n})-\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime}),\Delta_{n}\big\rangle-\frac{1}{n}\big\langle\nabla\ell(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime};Z_{n}^{\prime})-\nabla\ell(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime};Z_{n}),\Delta_{n}\big\rangle\geq 0.

Rearranging terms, we are left with

\big\langle\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime})-\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}),\Delta_{n}\big\rangle\leq\frac{1}{n}\,\big\langle\nabla\ell(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime};Z_{n})-\nabla\ell(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime};Z_{n}^{\prime}),\Delta_{n}\big\rangle.

(226)

According to Assumption
D.1

there exists a quantity
\beta_{L}>0
such that

\big\|\nabla\ell(\vartheta;z)-\nabla\ell(\vartheta;z^{\prime})\big\|_{2}\leq 2\beta_{L}\qquad\text{for all }\vartheta\in{\mathcal{C}},\;z,z^{\prime}.

(227)

With (
227
) in mind, we can bound the right-hand side of
(
226
) using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\,\big|\big\langle\nabla\ell(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime};Z_{n}^{\prime})-\nabla\ell(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime};Z_{n}),\Delta_{n}\big\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\,\big\|\nabla\ell(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime};Z_{n}^{\prime})-\nabla\ell(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime};Z_{n})\big\|_{2}\,\|\Delta_{n}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\beta_{L}}{n}\,\|\Delta_{n}\|_{2}.

Combine this with (
226
) to arrive at the upper bound

\big\langle\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime})-\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}),\Delta_{n}\big\rangle\leq\frac{2\beta_{L}}{n}\,\|\Delta_{n}\|_{2}.

(228)

On the other hand, the left-hand side of (
228
) can be expressed in terms of the empirical Hessian. Specifically, by the fundamental theorem of calculus for vector-valued functions,

\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime})-\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n})=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}\widehat{L}_{n}\big(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}+t\Delta_{n}\big)\,\Delta_{n}\,\mathrm{d}t,

and as a consequence,

\big\langle\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime})-\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}),\Delta_{n}\big\rangle=\int_{0}^{1}\Delta_{n}^{\top}\nabla^{2}\widehat{L}_{n}\big(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}+t\Delta_{n}\big)\Delta_{n}\,\mathrm{d}t.

In particular, there exists some
\widetilde{\vartheta}
lying within the line segment between
\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}
and
\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime}
such that

\big\langle\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime})-\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}),\Delta_{n}\big\rangle=\Delta_{n}^{\top}\nabla^{2}\widehat{L}_{n}(\widetilde{\vartheta})\Delta_{n}.

We now control the empirical Hessian
\nabla^{2}\widehat{L}_{n}(\vartheta)
uniformly over all
\vartheta\in{\mathcal{C}}
. Denote
{\mathcal{N}}\big(\frac{\mu}{4\beta_{s}},{\mathcal{C}}\big)
as the
\frac{\mu}{4}
-cover of
{\mathcal{C}}
. Then we have
|{\mathcal{N}}\big(\frac{\mu}{4\beta_{s}},{\mathcal{C}}\big)|\leq\Big(\frac{12\beta_{s}D_{\mathcal{C}}}{\mu}\Big)^{d}
. According to Bernstein’s inequality, we have

\sup_{\vartheta\in{\mathcal{N}}\big(\frac{\mu}{4\beta_{s}},{\mathcal{C}}\big)}\Big\{\big\|\nabla^{2}\widehat{L}_{n}(\vartheta)-\nabla^{2}L(\vartheta)\big\|_{\infty,\infty}\Big\}\leq\frac{\mu}{4}

(229)

with probability at least
1-d^{2}\exp\big\{-\frac{\mu^{2}n}{32\beta_{s}^{2}D_{\mathcal{C}}^{2}}\big\}
, provided that
n\geq\frac{32d\beta_{s}^{2}D_{\mathcal{C}}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\log\big(\frac{12\beta_{s}D_{\mathcal{C}}}{\mu}\big)
. Combining this with Assumption
D.1
leads to

\displaystyle\sup_{\vartheta\in{\mathcal{C}}}\Big\{\big\|\nabla^{2}\widehat{L}_{n}(\vartheta)-\nabla^{2}L(\vartheta)\big\|_{\infty,\infty}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq\beta_{s}\sup_{\vartheta\in{\mathcal{C}}}\inf_{\vartheta^{*}\in{\mathcal{N}}\big(\frac{\mu}{4\beta_{s}},{\mathcal{C}}\big)}\Big\{\|\,\vartheta-\vartheta^{*}\,\|_{2}\Big\}

\displaystyle\quad+\sup_{\vartheta^{*}\in{\mathcal{N}}\big(\frac{\mu}{4\beta_{s}},{\mathcal{C}}\big)}\Big\{\big\|\nabla^{2}\widehat{L}_{n}(\vartheta)-\nabla^{2}L(\vartheta)\big\|_{\infty,\infty}\Big\}\overset{\eqref{eq:hessian-concentration}}{\leq}\beta_{s}\frac{\mu}{4\beta_{s}}+\frac{\mu}{4}\leq\frac{\mu}{2}.

This combined with the strong convexity assumption and (
229
) tells us that: for all
\vartheta\in{\mathcal{C}}
and every unit vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

u^{\top}\nabla^{2}\widehat{L}_{n}(\vartheta)u\geq u^{\top}\nabla^{2}L(\vartheta)u-\big\|\nabla^{2}\widehat{L}_{n}(\vartheta)-\nabla^{2}L(\vartheta)\big\|\geq\mu-\frac{\mu}{2}=\frac{\mu}{2}.

Putting all these pieces together, we arrive at

\frac{\mu}{2}\|\Delta_{n}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\Delta_{n}^{\top}\nabla^{2}\widehat{L}_{n}(\widetilde{\vartheta})\Delta_{n}=\left\langle\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}^{\prime})-\nabla\widehat{L}_{n}(\widehat{\vartheta}_{n}),\Delta_{n}\right\rangle\leq\frac{2\beta_{L}}{n}\,\|\Delta_{n}\|_{2}.

If
\Delta_{n}=0
, the bound is trivial. Otherwise, cancelling
\|\Delta_{n}\|_{2}
from both sides yields

\|\Delta_{n}\|\leq\frac{4\beta_{L}}{\mu n}.

D.4.2
Proof of Proposition
D.2

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\vartheta_{1}=0
. Note that under the stepsize choice
\eta_{n}=\min\{1/\widehat{\sigma},\gamma_{n}/n\}
, it can be easily seen that: for any unit vector
u
,

1=\left\|{u}\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq u^{\top}(I-\eta_{n}\widehat{A}_{n})u\geq\left\|{u}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\eta_{n}\widehat{\sigma}=1-\eta_{n}\widehat{\sigma}\geq 0.

This implies that for any
n
,
\|I-\eta_{n}\widehat{A}_{n}\|\leq 1
.
According to the LSA update rule (
220
), we have

\displaystyle\|\vartheta_{n}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\big(I-\eta_{n-1}\widehat{A}_{n-1}\big)\vartheta_{n-1}\big\|_{2}+\eta_{n-1}\big\|\widehat{b}_{n}\big\|_{2}

(230)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(a)}}{\leq}\left\|{\vartheta_{n-1}}\right\|_{2}+\frac{CL\log(n-1)}{n-1}\leq\ldots\leq\sum\limits_{{i}={1}}^{n}\frac{CL\log i}{i}\leq CL\log^{2}n,

where we have made use of Assumption
D.2
and the choice that
\eta_{n}\leq\gamma_{n}/n
.

Stability at iteration
l
.

By construction,

\vartheta_{i}=\vartheta_{i}^{\prime}
holds for all
i=1,\dots,l.

At the perturbed iteration
l+1
, it holds that

\displaystyle\vartheta_{l+1}-\vartheta_{l+1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Bigl[\vartheta_{l}-\eta_{l}\bigl(\widehat{A}_{l}\vartheta_{l}-\widehat{b}_{l}\bigr)\Bigr]-\Bigl[\vartheta_{l}^{\prime}-\eta_{l}\bigl(\widehat{A}_{l}^{\prime}\vartheta_{l}^{\prime}-\widehat{b}_{l}^{\prime}\bigr)\Bigr]=-\eta_{l}\Bigl[(\widehat{A}_{l}-\widehat{A}_{l}^{\prime})\vartheta_{l}-(\widehat{b}_{l}-\widehat{b}_{l}^{\prime})\Bigr].

Taking this together with
\eta_{l}\leq{\gamma_{l}}/{l}
,
\sigma\geq 1
, and Assumption
D.2
leads to

\displaystyle\bigl\|\vartheta_{l+1}-\vartheta_{l+1}^{\prime}\bigr\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\gamma_{l}}{l}\left(\big\|\widetilde{A}_{l}\big\|+\big\|\widetilde{A}_{l}^{\prime}\big\|\right)\left\|{\vartheta_{l}}\right\|_{2}+\frac{2\gamma_{l}L}{l}

(231)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:conserv_bd_vartheta}}{\leq}\frac{2\gamma_{l}}{l}\left(\sigma CL\log^{2}l+L\right)\leq\frac{4C\sigma L\log^{3}l}{l}.

Error propagation after iteration
l
.

Let us define, for all
i\geq 1
,

\Delta_{i}\coloneqq\vartheta_{i}-\vartheta_{i}^{\prime}.

For every
i>l
, the two recursions share the same

(\widehat{A}_{i},\widehat{b}_{i})
, so subtracting their updates yields

\Delta_{i+1}=\bigl(I-\eta_{i}\widehat{A}_{i}\bigr)\Delta_{i}.

Iterating over
i=l,\dots,n
gives

\Delta_{n+1}=\Gamma_{l,n}^{\gamma}\Delta_{l+1},\qquad\Gamma_{l,n}^{\gamma}\coloneqq\prod_{i=l+1}^{n}\bigl(I-\eta_{i}\widehat{A}_{i}\bigr),

(232)

where the matrix product is ordered from
i=l+1
(the rightmost) up to
n
(the leftmost).
Combining (
231
) and (
232
) yields

\|\Delta_{n+1}\|_{2}=\|\Gamma_{l,n}^{\gamma}\Delta_{l+1}\|_{2}\leq\|\Gamma_{l,n}^{\gamma}\|\,\|\Delta_{l+1}\|_{2}\leq\frac{4C\sigma L\log^{3}l}{l}\,\|\Gamma_{l,n}^{\gamma}\|.

(233)

Hence, it boils down to
controlling the operator norm of the random matrix product

\Gamma_{l,n}^{\gamma}
.

Tools for analyzing products of random matrices.

To bound
\|\Gamma_{l,n}^{\gamma}\|
, we follow the framework of

33
;
46
. For any matrix

B\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, let
(\sigma_{\ell}(B))_{\ell=1}^{d}
denote its
singular values, and for
p\geq 1
define the Schatten
p
-norm

\|B\|_{p}\coloneqq\bigl(\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\sigma_{\ell}^{p}(B)\bigr)^{1/p}.

For
p,q\geq 1
and a random matrix
X
, define

\|X\|_{p,q}\coloneqq\bigl(\mathbb{E}[\|X\|_{p}^{q}]\bigr)^{1/q}.

We record the following two useful lemmas from
33
.

Lemma D.1

(Proposition 2 in
33
)
.

Let
\{Y_{\ell}:\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
be an independent sequence. Assume that for each
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exist

m_{\ell}\in(0,1)
and
\sigma_{\ell}>0
such that

\|\mathbb{E}[Y_{\ell}]\|^{2}\leq 1-m_{\ell}\quad\text{and}\quad\|Y_{\ell}-\mathbb{E}[Y_{\ell}]\|\leq\sigma_{\ell}\quad\text{almost surely}.

Define
Z_{n}=\prod_{\ell=0}^{n}Y_{\ell}=Y_{n}Z_{n-1}
for
n\geq 1
, with an
arbitrary starting point
Z_{0}
. Then, for any
2\leq q\leq p
and
n\geq 1
, one has

\|Z_{n}\|_{p,q}^{2}\leq\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}\bigl(1-m_{\ell}+(p-1)\sigma_{\ell}^{2}\bigr)\bigl\|Z_{0}\bigr\|_{p,q}^{2}.

Lemma D.2

(Lemma 1 in
33
)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}
,
B>0
,
C\geq 1
, and
p_{0},p_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
with

1\leq p_{0}\leq p_{1}<\infty
. Let
X
be a real random variable satisfying,
for any
p\in[p_{0},p_{1}]
,

\mathbb{E}[|X|^{p}]\leq C\exp(-Ap+Bp^{2}).

(234)

Then, for all
\delta\in(0,1]
, with probability at least
1-\delta
one has

|X|\leq\exp\Bigl(-A+Bp_{0}+2\sqrt{B\log(C/\delta)}+\frac{\log(C/\delta)}{p_{1}}\Bigr).

High-probability bound on
\|\Gamma_{l,n}^{\gamma}\|
.

We intend to apply Lemma
D.1
to analyze the matrix product

\Gamma_{j,n}^{\gamma}\coloneqq\prod_{i=j}^{n}\bigl(I-\eta_{i}A_{i}+\eta_{i}\widetilde{A}_{i}\bigr),\qquad j\leq n.

Take
Y_{i}=I-\eta_{i}A_{i}+\eta_{i}\widetilde{A}_{i}
, and
Z_{0}=I_{d}
. Then
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}]=I-\eta_{i}A_{i}
and

Y_{i}-\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}]=\eta_{i}\widetilde{A}_{i}
.
With Assumption
D.2
in place, by setting

m_{i}=\mu\eta_{i},\qquad\sigma_{i}=\sigma\eta_{i},

we see that
\|\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}]\|^{2}\leq 1-m_{i}
and

\|Y_{i}-\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}]\|\leq\sigma_{i}
.
Lemma
D.1
then tells us that, for any
2\leq q\leq p
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\|\Gamma_{j,n}^{\gamma}\|_{p}^{q}\bigr]^{1/q}

\displaystyle=\|\Gamma_{j,n}^{\gamma}\|_{p,q}\leq\prod_{i=j}^{n}\Bigl(1-\mu\eta_{i}+(p-1)\sigma^{2}\eta_{i}^{2}\Bigr)\|I_{d}\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq d^{1/p}\exp\left\{-\mu\sum_{i=j}^{n}\eta_{i}+(p-1)\sigma^{2}\sum_{i=j}^{n}\eta_{i}^{2}\right\},

(235)

where the last step makes use of the elementary inequality
\log(1+x)\leq x
.

Since

\|I-\eta_{i}\widehat{A}_{i}\|\leq 1
for each
i
, we have

\|\Gamma_{l,n}^{\gamma}\|\leq 1
for all
l\leq n
.
By introducing an index

j\coloneqq\max\Bigl\{l,\bigl\lceil 12C\sigma^{2}/\mu\bigr\rceil,\bigl\lceil 2C\widehat{\sigma}\log(C\widehat{\sigma})\bigr\rceil\Bigr\},

we can easily check that
\eta_{i}={\gamma_{i}}/{i}
for every
i\geq j
.
Further, we can derive the following inequality:

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}\sum\limits_{{i}={j}}^{n}\frac{\gamma_{i}^{2}}{i^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}\sigma^{2}\log^{2}n\left(\frac{1}{j-1}-\frac{1}{n}\right)\leq\frac{C\mu}{12}\log^{2}n

(236)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(a)}}{\leq}\frac{\mu}{6}\int_{j}^{n}\frac{C\log x}{x}{\textnormal{d}}x\leq\frac{\mu}{6}\sum\limits_{{i}={j}}^{n}\frac{C\log i}{i}=\frac{\mu}{6}\sum\limits_{{i}={j}}^{n}\frac{\gamma_{i}}{i}.

Here, (a) holds whenever
n\geq 3j
. In the regime
n\leq j
, the desired claim follows directly by combining (
233
) with the bound
\left\|{\Gamma_{l,n}^{\gamma}}\right\|\leq 1
.
Taking
p=q
in (
235
), we obtain

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\|\Gamma_{j,n}^{\gamma}\|_{p}^{p}\bigr]\leq d\exp\left\{-p\mu\sum_{i=j}^{n}\frac{\gamma_{i}}{i}+p^{2}\sigma^{2}\sum_{i=j}^{n}\frac{\gamma_{i}^{2}}{i^{2}}\right\}.

(237)

Applying Lemma
D.2
with

p_{0}=2
,
p_{1}=\infty
,
C=d
,

A=\mu\sum_{i=j}^{n}\frac{\gamma_{i}}{i},~B=\sigma^{2}\sum_{i=j}^{n}\frac{\gamma_{i}^{2}}{i^{2}},

and using the fact that (
237
) implies
(
234
) for all
p\geq 2
, we can deduce that, with probability at
least
1-\delta/n
,

\displaystyle\|\Gamma_{j,n}^{\gamma}\|

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left\{-\mu\sum_{i=j}^{n}\frac{\gamma_{i}}{i}+3\sigma^{2}\sum_{i=j}^{n}\frac{\gamma_{i}^{2}}{i^{2}}+\log\frac{dn}{\delta}\right\}\overset{\eqref{eq:lsa_absorb_high_order}}{\leq}\exp\left\{-\frac{\mu}{2}\sum_{i=j}^{n}\frac{\gamma_{i}}{i}+\log\frac{dn}{\delta}\right\}.

Using the elementary fact
\sum_{i=j}^{n}\frac{\log i}{i}\geq\log^{2}n-\log^{2}j=\log(n/j)\log(nj)\geq\log(n/j)\log n
, we further obtain

\|\Gamma_{j,n}^{\gamma}\|\leq\frac{dn}{\delta}\Bigl(\frac{j}{n}\Bigr)^{\frac{C\mu\log n}{2}}.

(238)

Since
\|\Gamma_{l,n}^{\gamma}\|\leq\|\Gamma_{j,n}^{\gamma}\|
holds by
construction and
\|\Gamma_{l,n}^{\gamma}\|\leq 1
, combining
(
238
) with the trivial upper bound 1 yields, for all

l\leq n
and with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\|\Gamma_{l,n}^{\gamma}\|\leq\min\left\{1,\,\frac{dn}{\delta}\left(\frac{\max\{l,\lceil 12C\sigma^{2}/\mu\rceil,\bigl\lceil 2C\widehat{\sigma}\log(C\widehat{\sigma})\bigr\rceil\}}{n}\right)^{\frac{C\mu\log n}{2}}\right\},\quad l=1,\ldots,n.

(239)

Bounded-difference property of
\vartheta_{n+1}
.

For ease of exposition, we introduce the following notation:

C_{0}\coloneqq\max\left\{\lceil 12C\sigma^{2}/\mu\rceil,\bigl\lceil 2C\widehat{\sigma}\log(C\widehat{\sigma})\bigr\rceil\right\}.

Substituting (
239
) into
(
233
), we arrive at

\|\Delta_{n+1}\|_{2}\leq\frac{4C\sigma L\log^{3}l}{l}\min\left\{1,\,\frac{dn}{\delta}\Bigl(\frac{\max\{l,C_{0}\}}{n}\Bigr)^{\frac{C\mu\log n}{2}}\right\}

(240)

holds for any
l=1,\ldots,n
.
Let
\kappa\coloneqq\sigma/\mu
and choose

\delta=n^{-\zeta},~C=\frac{2(\zeta+1)}{\mu}

. For
l\leq C_{0}
, use (
240
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta_{n+1}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4C\sigma L\log^{3}C_{0}\min\left\{1,dn^{\zeta+1}\left(\frac{C_{0}}{n}\right)^{(\zeta+1)\log n}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(a)}}{\leq}4C\sigma L\log^{3}C_{0}\left(\frac{dC_{0}^{(\zeta+1)\log n}}{n^{(\zeta+1)(\log n-1)}}\right)^{\frac{1}{(\zeta+1)(\log n-1)}}\overset{\mathrm{(b)}}{\leq}4C\sigma L\log^{3}C_{0}\frac{eC_{0}^{2}}{n}.

Here, (a) results from the fact that
a\leq a^{\lambda}
for any
a,\lambda\in[0,1]
, and (b) holds since
d^{\frac{1}{(\zeta+1)(\log n-1)}}\leq e
provided that
n^{\zeta}\geq d
. Thus, in this setting,
Proposition

D.2
holds by taking
K={8e(\zeta+1)\kappa LC_{0}^{2}\log^{3}C_{0}}
.

For
l>C_{0}
, similarly we can derive

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta_{n+1}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4C\sigma L\log^{3}n}{l}\min\left\{1,dn^{\zeta+1}\left(\frac{l}{n}\right)^{(\zeta+1)\log n}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4C\sigma L\log^{3}n}{l}\left(\frac{dl^{(\zeta+1)\log n}}{n^{(\zeta+1)(\log n-1)}}\right)^{\frac{1}{(\zeta+1)(\log n-1)}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4eC\sigma L\log^{3}n}{n}\cdot l^{\frac{(\zeta+1)\log n}{(\zeta+1)(\log n-1)}-1}\leq\frac{4eC\sigma L\log^{3}n}{n}\cdot n^{\frac{1}{\log n-1}}\leq\frac{16eC\sigma L\log^{3}n}{n}.

In this setting,
Proposition

D.2
follows by taking
K=16eC\sigma L
.

D.4.3
Proof of Proposition
D.3

We let
K\coloneqq\frac{2BL\gamma}{\mu}
and prove this result by induction. Fix two adjacent data streams, and let

\{\vartheta_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
and
\{\vartheta_{k}^{\prime}\}_{k\geq 1}
denote the iterates generated by
(
221
) from the same initialization. Assume for the moment that

\displaystyle\|\vartheta_{n}-\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq\min\left\{\frac{K}{n},\frac{2B}{L}\right\}

(241)

holds for some

n\geq 1
, and
we would like to bound
\|\vartheta_{n+1}-\vartheta_{n+1}^{\prime}\|_{2}
.

We divide into several cases according to the index at which the two data streams differ. First, consider the case where the two streams differ at the most recent observation, i.e.,

(X_{n},Y_{n})\neq(X_{n}^{\prime},Y_{n}^{\prime})
while
(X_{i},Y_{i})=(X_{i}^{\prime},Y_{i}^{\prime})
for all
i\leq n-1
.
In this case, the iterates coincide up to time
n
, hence
\vartheta_{n}=\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}
.
In view of the update rule (
221
),

\displaystyle\|\vartheta_{n+1}-\vartheta_{n+1}^{\prime}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\eta_{n}\Bigl\|f\bigl(\vartheta_{n};(X_{n},Y_{n})\bigr)-f\bigl(\vartheta_{n};(X_{n}^{\prime},Y_{n}^{\prime})\bigr)\Bigr\|_{2}

\displaystyle{\leq}\min\left\{\frac{2\gamma B}{n},~\frac{2B}{L}\right\}\leq\min\left\{\frac{K}{n+1},\frac{2B}{L}\right\},

where penultimate inequality arises from Assumption
D.3
and our stepsize choice, and the last inequality holds since

\frac{2\gamma B}{n}\leq\frac{4\gamma B}{n+1}\leq\frac{2BL\gamma}{\mu(n+1)}=\frac{K}{n+1}.

Next, consider the case where the two data streams differ at some index
l\leq n-1
. Then the current update at
time
n
is computed from the same observation
Z_{n}=(X_{n},Y_{n})
in both streams.
Since
\eta_{n}\leq 1/L
,
50
implies that the update map is nonexpansive, which, taken together with the induction hypothesis (
241
), yields

\|\vartheta_{n+1}-\vartheta_{n+1}^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq\|\vartheta_{n}-\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq\min\Bigl\{\frac{K}{n},\,\frac{2B}{L}\Bigr\}.

In addition, a direct expansion of the recursion yields

\displaystyle\|\vartheta_{n+1}-\vartheta_{n+1}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\bigl\|\vartheta_{n}-\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}-\eta_{n}\bigl(f(\vartheta_{n};Z_{n})-f(\vartheta_{n}^{\prime};Z_{n})\bigr)\bigr\|_{2}^{2}

(242)

\displaystyle\leq\|\vartheta_{n}-\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}-2\eta_{n}\left\langle f(\vartheta_{n};Z_{n})-f(\vartheta_{n}^{\prime};Z_{n}),\vartheta_{n}-\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}\right\rangle+L^{2}\eta_{n}^{2}\|\vartheta_{n}-\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\mu\eta_{n}+L^{2}\eta_{n}^{2}\right)\|\vartheta_{n}-\vartheta_{n}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{K^{2}\left(1-2\mu\eta_{n}+L^{2}\eta_{n}^{2}\right)}{n^{2}},

which follows from Assumption
D.3
. Further, it can be derived that

\displaystyle\left(1-2\mu\eta_{n}+L^{2}\eta_{n}^{2}\right)\frac{(n+1)^{2}}{n^{2}}\overset{\mathrm{(a)}}{\leq}(1-\mu\eta_{n})(1+3/n)\overset{\mathrm{(b)}}{\leq}1,

where (a) is valid provided that
n+1\geq\frac{L^{2}\gamma}{\mu}
and (b) holds as long as
\gamma\geq{3}/{\mu}
. Substitution into (
242
) yields

\left\|{\vartheta_{n+1}-\vartheta_{n+1}^{\prime}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{K^{2}}{(n+1)^{2}}.

Moreover, if
n+1<\frac{L^{2}\gamma}{\mu}
, then it still holds that

\left\|{\vartheta_{n+1}-\vartheta_{n+1}^{\prime}}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{2B}{L}\leq\frac{2BL^{2}\gamma}{\mu L(n+1)}=\frac{K}{n+1}.

We have thus concluded the proof of Proposition
D.3
.

Appendix E
Auxiliary concentration inequalities

This appendix collects several classical concentration inequalities that will be used repeatedly in our analysis.
Their proofs can be found in, e.g.,
22
;
75
.

Lemma E.1

(McDiarmid inequality)
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be independent random variables taking values in measurable spaces
\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{n}
, and let
f:\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function satisfying the bounded differences condition: there exist constants
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\geq 0
such that for all
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},x_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{i}
,

\bigl|f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i},\ldots,x_{n})-f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i}^{\prime},\ldots,x_{n})\bigr|\leq c_{i}.

Then, for all
t>0
,

{\mathbb{P}}\!\left(\bigl|f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})-{\mathbb{E}}\big[f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\big]\bigr|\geq t\right)\;\leq\;2\exp\!\left(-\frac{2t^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}^{2}}\right).

Lemma E.2

(Khintchine inequality for
p=1
)
.

Let
\{\varepsilon_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be independent Rademacher random variables,
that is,
{\mathbb{P}}(\varepsilon_{i}=1)={\mathbb{P}}(\varepsilon_{i}=-1)=1/2
.
Then for any real coefficients
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
, one has

{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\bigg|\bigg]\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}.

Lemma E.3

(Paley–Zygmund inequality)
.

Let
Z
be a nonnegative random variable with
{\mathbb{E}}[Z^{2}]<\infty
.
Then, for any
\theta\in[0,1]
, the following inequality holds:

{\mathbb{P}}\big(Z\geq\theta\,{\mathbb{E}}[Z]\big)\;\geq\;(1-\theta)^{2}\frac{({\mathbb{E}}[Z])^{2}}{{\mathbb{E}}[Z^{2}]}.

Lemma E.4

(Generalized DKW inequality)
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be independent random elements taking values in
{\mathcal{X}}
.
Let
F:{\mathcal{X}}\times\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
satisfy that, for every
u\in{\mathcal{X}}
, the map
x\mapsto F(u,x)
is
nondecreasing and right-continuous.
Define

\widehat{F}_{n}(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}F(X_{i},x),\qquad\overline{F}(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\big[F(X_{i},x)\big].

Then for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\bigl|\widehat{F}_{n}(x)-\overline{F}(x)\bigr|\leq\frac{4}{\sqrt{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{2n}}.

Specifically, when
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}={\mathbb{R}},~i=1,\ldots,n
and
F(X_{i},x)=\mathbbm{1}\{X_{i}\leq x\}
we have:

{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup\limits_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}\left|{\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{{i}={1}}^{n}\bigl(\mathbbm{1}\{X_{i}\leq x\}-{\mathbb{P}}(X_{i}\leq x)\bigr)}\right|\leq\frac{4}{\sqrt{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{2n}}\right)\geq 1-\delta.

Proof of
Lemma

E.4
.

The proof comprises the following steps.

Step 1: applying McDiarmid around the mean.

Let

g\coloneqq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\bigl|\widehat{F}_{n}(x)-\overline{F}(x)\bigr|.

Fix any
i\in[n]
. Replace
X_{i}
by an arbitrary
\widetilde{X}_{i}
, and define

\widetilde{F}_{n}(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\Big(F(\widetilde{X}_{i},x)+\sum_{j\neq i}F(X_{j},x)\Big),\qquad\widetilde{g}\coloneqq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\bigl|\widetilde{F}_{n}(x)-\overline{F}(x)\bigr|.

Since
0\leq F\leq 1
, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\bigl|\widehat{F}_{n}(x)-\widetilde{F}_{n}(x)\bigr|=\frac{1}{n}\bigl|F(X_{i},x)-F(\widetilde{X}_{i},x)\bigr|\leq\frac{1}{n}.

Using
\left|{\left|{u}\right|-\left|{v}\right|}\right|\leq\left|{u-v}\right|
and

\left|{\sup_{x}a(x)-\sup_{x}b(x)}\right|\leq\sup_{x}\left|{a(x)-b(x)}\right|
, we obtain

|g-\widetilde{g}|\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\bigl|\widehat{F}_{n}(x)-\widetilde{F}_{n}(x)\bigr|\leq\frac{1}{n},

so
g
satisfies bounded differences with constants
c_{i}=1/n
.
By McDiarmid’s inequality, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

g\leq\mathbb{E}[g]+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{2n}}.

(243)

Step 2: controlling
\mathbb{E}[g]
via symmetrization and a reduction to indicators.

Introduce a ghost sample
X_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{n}^{\prime}
, independent of

(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
satisfying
X_{i}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{d}}}{{=}}X_{i}
, and let
\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}
be i.i.d. Rademacher
signs, independent of everything else. Recognizing that
\mathbb{E}[F(X_{i},x)]=\mathbb{E}[F(X_{i}^{\prime},x)]
, we can express

\widehat{F}_{n}(x)-\overline{F}(x)=\mathbb{E}_{X^{\prime}}\!\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(F(X_{i},x)-F(X_{i}^{\prime},x)\bigr)\,\middle|\,X\right].

By the Jensen inequality and the convexity of
\sup
, we have

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\widehat{F}_{n}(x)-\overline{F}(x)\big|\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{X,X^{\prime}}\!\left[\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(F(X_{i},x)-F(X_{i}^{\prime},x)\bigr)\right|\right].

Using exchangeability of
(X_{i},X_{i}^{\prime})
to insert Rademacher signs and then invoking the triangle inequality gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X,X^{\prime}}\!\left[\sup_{x}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(F(X_{i},x)-F(X_{i}^{\prime},x)\bigr)\right|\right]=\mathbb{E}_{X,X^{\prime},\epsilon}\!\left[\sup_{x}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\bigl(F(X_{i},x)-F(X_{i}^{\prime},x)\bigr)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{X,X^{\prime},\epsilon}\!\left[\sup_{x}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}F(X_{i},x)\right|\right]+\mathbb{E}_{X,X^{\prime},\epsilon}\!\left[\sup_{x}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}F(X_{i}^{\prime},x)\right|\right]=2\,\mathbb{E}_{X,\epsilon}\!\left[\sup_{x}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}F(X_{i},x)\right|\right].

Taking the above inequalities together yields

\mathbb{E}[g]=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|{\widehat{F}_{n}(x)-\overline{F}(x)}\right|\right]\leq\frac{2}{n}\mathbb{E}_{X,\epsilon}\Big[\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}F(X_{i},x)\Big|\Big].

(244)

We next bound the Rademacher term.
Since
0\leq F(X_{i},x)\leq 1
, for each
i
and
x
,

F(X_{i},x)=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbbm{1}\{F(X_{i},x)\geq t\}\,{\textnormal{d}}t.

Therefore, for any fixed
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}F(X_{i},x)\Big|=\Big|\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{F(X_{i},x)\geq t\}\,{\textnormal{d}}t\Big|\leq\int_{0}^{1}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{F(X_{i},x)\geq t\}\Big|\,{\textnormal{d}}t,

and hence

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}F(X_{i},x)\Big|\leq\int_{0}^{1}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{F(X_{i},x)\geq t\}\Big|\,{\textnormal{d}}t.

(245)

Fix
t\in[0,1]
. For any
u\in{\mathcal{X}}
, define the threshold

a(u,t)\coloneqq\inf\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\ F(u,x)\geq t\}\in[-\infty,+\infty].

Since
x\mapsto F(u,x)
is nondecreasing and right-continuous, we have

\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\ F(u,x)\geq t\}=[a(u,t),\infty),

and thus, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbbm{1}\{F(X_{i},x)\geq t\}=\mathbbm{1}\{x\geq a(X_{i},t)\}.

(246)

Consequently,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{F(X_{i},x)\geq t\}\Big|=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{x\geq a(X_{i},t)\}\Big|.

Let
a_{(1)}(t)\leq\cdots\leq a_{(n)}(t)
be the order statistics of
\{a(X_{i},t)\}_{i=1}^{n}
,
and let
\epsilon_{(1)}(t),\ldots,\epsilon_{(n)}(t)
be the corresponding reordered signs.
Then the mapping
x\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{x\geq a(X_{i},t)\}
is piecewise constant and,
as
x
increases, it takes values
\sum_{j=1}^{k}\epsilon_{(j)}(t)
for some
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
. Hence,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{x\geq a(X_{i},t)\}\Big|=\max_{0\leq k\leq n}\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{k}\epsilon_{(j)}(t)\Big|.

Since
(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})
are i.i.d. and independent of the
X_{i}
’s, conditionally on
X_{1:n}
the reordered sequence

(\epsilon_{(1)}(t),\ldots,\epsilon_{(n)}(t))
has the same joint distribution as
(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})
.
Therefore,

\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\Big[\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{F(X_{i},x)\geq t\}\Big|\ \Big|\ X_{1:n}\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\Big[\max_{0\leq k\leq n}\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{k}\epsilon_{j}\Big|\Big].

Let
S_{k}\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{k}\epsilon_{j}
,
k=0,1,\ldots,n
. Then
(S_{k})_{k=0}^{n}
is a martingale, and
Doob’s
L^{2}
maximal inequality yields

\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{0\leq k\leq n}|S_{k}|^{2}\Big]\leq 4\mathbb{E}\big[|S_{n}|^{2}\big]=4n.

By Cauchy–Schwarz,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{0\leq k\leq n}|S_{k}|\Big]\leq 2\sqrt{n}.

Combining this with (
245
) and applying Tonelli’s theorem gives

\mathbb{E}_{X,\epsilon}\Big[\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}F(X_{i},x)\Big|\Big]\leq\int_{0}^{1}2\sqrt{n}\,{\textnormal{d}}t=2\sqrt{n}.

Substituting into (
244
) yields

\mathbb{E}[g]\leq\frac{2}{n}\cdot 2\sqrt{n}=\frac{4}{\sqrt{n}}.

(247)

Step 3: completing the proof.

Substituting (
247
) into (
243
) completes the proof.
∎
